Abstract (英文): Web platforms run on multivariate time series, from service telemetry and Web traffic logs to sensor streams served through Web APIs. Yet, retention policies often archive them at coarse granularity to save storage and bandwidth. Decisions such as autoscaling, alerting, and billing operate at finer granularity than existing forecasters reach, since these models predict at the resolution of their input. We therefore introduce Multivariate Time Series Super-Resolution Forecasting (MTS-SRF), which forecasts fine-grained futures from coarse histories, and propose TimeMagnifier to address its three challenges. Fourier Anchor counters data sparsity by fitting each input window to a trend-Fourier structure with sparse cross-variable gating; De-noise Only constrains fine-grained ambiguity by refining along the resolution axis rather than the noise axis, lengthening the grid level by level; and banded DTW Alignment corrects cross-resolution misalignment against the coarse forecast. On the first MTS-SRF benchmark, with ten datasets from five domains, real archived cross-resolution pairs, strengthened baseline adaptations, and five-seed significance tests, TimeMagnifier is best on every dataset, resolution coefficient, and metric against 15 baselines and cuts MSE by 12.3% on average. Its De-noise Only and DTW stages also transfer as a plug-in that lowers the MSE of existing forecasters by 5.1% to 9.0%. Code will be made publicly available.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Skill learning enables web agents to reuse prior experience for long-horizon interactive tasks. However, learned skills are typically evaluated in closed environments, overlooking the fact that real-world web deployment involves continuous shifts in invocation context, perceptual grounding, and execution feasibility. To this end, we introduce SkillShift-Bench, the first benchmark for evaluating skill learning with controlled open-environment shifts. SkillShift-Bench simulates Contextual Shift, Perceptual Shift, and Execution Shift across three self-hosted web systems, and evaluates agents with diagnostics covering task success, interaction cost, skill use, and skill failure. Comprehensive experiments reveal systematic robustness degradation under controlled shifts, characterized by lower success rates, higher interaction costs, and more frequent skill failures, with Execution Shift exerting the strongest pressure. We further formulate shifted skill execution as a nonstationary local executability problem and provide both a dynamic-regret analysis and a practical test-time adaptation method, demonstrating the feasibility of improving learned-skill execution without relearning the skill library. Code, tasks, environments, documentation, and the leaderboard are available at https://skillshift-bench.github.io/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Financial fraud caused over \$15.9 billion in losses in the United States in 2025, according to the Federal Trade Commission~\citep{ftc2026databook}. Understanding such large-scale harm requires jointly modeling informational and financial dynamics. Yet existing simulators typically capture only one side of this interaction, leaving a gap in modeling how fraud unfolds in the real world. To bridge this gap, we introduce \textsc{FraudSociety}, the first LLM-agent-based simulator that jointly models these coupled dynamics. It integrates a dynamic social environment for information exposure and communication, persistent financial ledgers that preserve payment and loss histories, and a Need-Intent-Action architecture for civilian decision making. Fraudsters further adapt their communication and financial actions to individual interaction histories and ledger states, forming a closed feedback loop between the two dynamics. We validate \textsc{FraudSociety} across macro-level outcomes, micro-level behavior, and intervention effects. At the macro level, the simulated fraud outcomes closely match real-world patterns across multiple key statistics. At the micro level, it reproduces strong loss concentration, with the top 10\% of victimization records accounting for 81.1\% of total losses, consistent with Canadian Anti-Fraud Centre statistics~\citep{cafc2025annual}. Counter-fraud experiments further reveal variation across intervention strategies, with the best-performing personalized intervention reducing the repeat-payment rate from 49.4\% to 10.9\%. These results establish \textsc{FraudSociety} as both a high-fidelity simulator and a scalable testbed for evaluating anti-fraud strategies under diverse intervention conditions, thereby advancing research on financial fraud and its prevention.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across diverse tasks. Yet their memory and collaboration architectures often remain static, constraining continued adaptation and improvement over time. Recent work addresses this limitation by evolving memory or collaboration based on interaction experience. However, updates to these interdependent components are not necessarily aligned: an evolved workflow may require experience that memory does not provide, while evolved memory may encode guidance that the workflow cannot use. To address this problem, we propose CoEvolve, a skill-centered framework for aligned memory--collaboration evolution. CoEvolve represents reusable local collaboration patterns as skills that link evolving collaboration decisions to their supporting memories. It further compares original and counterfactual trajectories to identify local evolution signals, which coordinate updates to the skill library, skill memory, and executor memories across rounds. Experiments on the ALFWorld and WebShop benchmarks confirm that CoEvolve achieves the best overall performance among the evaluated baselines, while ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of aligned co-evolution and counterfactual-guided evolution.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-agent systems have emerged as a promising paradigm for multimodal medical reasoning, but existing methods rely on two assumptions that hinder practical deployment: (i) always-active dense collaboration among multiple agents, and (ii) dependence on large cloud-based models that raise privacy and efficiency concerns. In this paper, we challenge both assumptions and propose SMART-Med, a fully local multi-agent method that treats collaboration as a learnable decision rather than a default behavior. Our key insight is that not every medical query requires costly multi-agent deliberation: many can be reliably solved by a single well-trained agent, while only uncertain cases benefit from expert collaboration. Based on this insight, SMART-Med introduces an uncertainty-driven routing mechanism that adaptively decides when to answer directly and when to recruit a small subset of expert agents, effectively converting dense multi-agent reasoning into selective, query-adaptive coordination. To make this routing reliable, we curate a new multimodal medical dataset MedVR-3K with high-quality reasoning traces and a difficulty hierarchy, and then fine-tune local agents using GRPO with two complementary rewards: a certainty-aware accuracy reward and a vision-language reward model for correctness and reasoning quality respectively. Experiments on four medical visual question answering benchmarks show that SMART-Med achieves competitive performance compared to existing MAS approaches while substantially reducing inference cost (reducing inference time by more than 3.8x and token usage by 72%). Moreover, after fine-tuning on MedVR-3K, SMART-Med yields an average improvement of 6.7% across Slake, PathVQA, VQA-RAD, and PMC-VQA.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Augmented large language model (LLM) systems have reported high benchmark accuracies in rare disease diagnosis, yet their real-world clinical utility remains unproven. A recent meta-analysis flagged augmented LLM diagnostic evaluations as having a high risk of bias due to suspected data leakage; however, the precise impact of these vulnerabilities on model inference has yet to be fully quantified. In this study, we present a comprehensive empirical audit to quantify evaluation artifacts across prevailing AI paradigms, using a repository of over 60,000 cases from diverse public and independent clinical cohorts. By reconstructing a state-of-the-art agentic framework, we demonstrate that candidate-space contamination artificially inflates performance: enforcing strict cross-dataset isolation resulted in a marked performance decline, with the cross-dataset mean Recall@1 decreasing from 54.3% (under conventional one-vs-rest evaluation) to 40.3% (Hedges' g = 0.94). Similar spatial overfitting in supervised classifiers caused diagnostic accuracy to drop from an internal mean of 42.9% to 16.7% across external cohorts (Cohen's d = 2.01). Furthermore, we quantify how LLMs infer diagnoses by exploiting retrospective therapeutic cues in clinical narratives, and how temporal misalignment in foundational knowledge bases leads to performance fluctuations. We conclude that prevailing evaluation protocols for augmented LLM systems inadvertently reflect data leakage rather than genuine medical reasoning. To address these systematic artifacts, we propose a rigorous evaluation framework - coupling strict data-isolation protocols with standardized benchmarking safeguards - to ensure future diagnostic AI for rare diseases is evaluated on actual clinical robustness.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Deployed machine learning (ML) models can degrade as the distribution of their input data shifts. Such shifts can result from changes in the population or from data integrity issues in the pipeline that materializes model features, two sources that operate through different mechanisms. Because labels are often absent or delayed, it is common practice to track shifts in the data instead, but a detected shift does not indicate how it affects the model. We formulate this problem as online model testing under distribution shift. Testing requires reproducing the shift on a controlled baseline with known labels. Since the same shift can come from either source, doing so requires knowing which mechanism changed. We propose a methodology that reproduces observed changes in both mechanisms to estimate current performance and the potential gain from repairing observed data integrity issues. Experiments on real-world datasets with simulated faults show that separating the two sources generally improves both estimates, supporting this formulation. A churn prediction case illustrates how these tests can support monitoring and repair prioritization

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) increasingly produce online articles from material that later becomes input to other models. This circulation connects individual responses to the information available for subsequent generation. We study a consequential distinction: an article can avoid endorsing a false claim while preserving the concrete details that enable its reproduction. Our framework connects claim content, expressed stance, and downstream reuse to explain selective error persistence under a continuing supply of accurate and inaccurate information. We implement a controlled community writing process that varies whether published articles return as inputs, matching external supply, input capacity, and generation volume. Across twelve factual targets and four production models, recirculation increases the presence of complete false claims by 7.29 percentage points at the final round while reducing explicit endorsement by 8.33 points. Effects differ across facts and models. Paired interventions then abstract eligible error details within fixed histories while preserving correct content and the surrounding controversy. Error reproduction remains largely unchanged across the evaluation targets, while a development quotation target exhibits local reductions. These findings connect persistent errors to the information available throughout a writing process and motivate evaluation of both what models affirm and what their publications supply to future writers.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Graph Neural Networks are widely used to learn structural representations of relational data. Hierarchical graph pooling learns multi-level representations by progressively coarsening graphs; however, prior methods rely on predefined cluster numbers or fixed pooling ratios, limiting adaptability to individual graphs. We propose Graph-Dependent Pooling (GDPool), which dynamically determines pooling granularity for each graph. We analyze optimization conflicts among layer-wise objectives through gradient alignment, showing that independently supervised pooling levels can send conflicting gradients to shared parameters. GDPool uses self-attention to select graph-specific anchors representing structurally important nodes and constructs coarse graphs through soft node assignment. Experiments on metal-organic framework and molecular graph benchmarks show that GDPool consistently achieves the best predictive performance across all benchmarks. Evaluations using modularity and the effective number of clusters show that GDPool achieves consistently higher modularity than existing methods while the number of active clusters decreases monotonically with depth. Sensitivity analyses confirm stable behavior across hyperparameter and architectural choices.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-Head Latent Attention (MLA) has been adopted by recent frontier large language models. Instead of per-head keys and values, MLA caches a compressed KV latent that is up-projected into keys and values at decode time, together with a narrow positional key that carries position information into the attention score. Existing KV-cache quantization methods are designed around the keys and values of standard attention and do not account for what MLA actually caches, which costs accuracy at low bit widths. We analyze the MLA cache both in activation distribution and in how quantization error propagates to the attention output, and show that the two cached tensors play different roles: the latent mainly supplies the values, whereas the positional key dominates the attention score. We therefore propose RAQ-MLA (Role-Aware Quantization for Multi-Head Latent Attention Caches), which fits an invertible transform to each cached tensor against the error that tensor delivers to the attention output: attention-weighted value error for the latent and score perturbation for the positional key. Without training, RAQ-MLA quantizes the full MLA cache to about 2.5 bits per element, a 6.5x increase in theoretical KV-cache capacity, while remaining nearly lossless on reasoning and coding tasks across four MLA models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Research ideas are always proposed under resource constraints: a plan must fit the available compute, API credit, and time. Yet evaluations of research-ideation agents mostly score unconstrained novelty or overlap with a published paper, and their LLM judges are rarely checked against peer review. We introduce AlphaIdeaBench, a benchmark of 100 ideation tasks (50 workshop calls for papers and 50 open problems stated in surveys), each paired with an explicit resource budget and a shared proposal schema. An LLM judge scores each proposal on 12 dimensions covering validity, novelty, and feasibility. To validate the judge, we compare its scores on four novelty axes (problem, method, scenario, and insight) with scores derived from reviewer comments on 600 papers from ICLR 2026, NeurIPS 2025, and ICML 2025. For problem and method novelty, Gemini 3.7 Flash is within one point of the reviewer-derived score on 94-97% of papers; agreement on scenario novelty is relatively lower. We evaluate eight published harnesses on a shared backbone and, within one harness, eight backbone LLMs. CoI-Agent obtains the highest overall score, narrowly ahead of SciMON, which leads on method and insight novelty. All harnesses score low on scenario novelty and rarely change the problem setting. The backbone largely determines which open problem a proposal targets, whereas the harness determines whether a proposal addresses one problem or combines several. Weaker backbones such as Llama 4 Scout fail to carry out that harness's strategy and score low on budget feasibility. Finally, when only the budget is varied on three topics, a larger budget changes the experimental setting on compute-intensive topics but only adds tests on an inexpensive one. We release the tasks, rubrics, and scores to foster standardized, reproducible benchmarking of future research-ideation agents under constraints.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Online distillation of vision-language-action (VLA) policies commonly relies on teacher action scores that an inference-only interface may not expose. Trajectory-only Adversarial Distillation (\gad{}) instead turns a fixed bank of teacher joint-position features into adaptive feedback: a discriminator compares feature marginals from teacher and student rollouts and supplies rewards for on-policy learning. The student interacts with the environment without online teacher queries or environment task rewards. On ManiSkill \texttt{PutOnPlateInScene25Main}, \gad{} improves OpenVLA-OFT (7B) from $26.2\%$ to $76.6\%$ \successonce{} ($+50.4$ percentage points) and from $20.3\%$ to $58.6\%$ \successend{}. It trails white-box on-policy distillation (OPD) at $81.6\%$ / $59.4\%$ by $5.0$ / $0.8$ percentage points ($n{=}256$ evaluation trajectories per checkpoint). The tested four-epoch continuation achieves the highest \gad{} result without additional teacher data. This best run uses $5\times$ OPD's student interaction budget and $12.5\times$ its complete-rollout batch-passes. The comparison demonstrates online VLA improvement without teacher scoring while quantifying unequal interaction and optimization budgets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Do preferences Large Language Models (LLMs) express in standard elicitation tasks predict their strategic behaviour? LLM agents interacting with humans and other AI systems face potentially conflicting objectives, such as individual welfare, group welfare, and environmental concerns. Prior work characterizes LLM preferences through behavioral-economic and psychological elicitation or strategic games, but rarely tests consistency across settings and prediction in other strategic environments. This requires modeling how competing objectives jointly shape behavior, whereas existing approaches typically measure preference dimensions in isolation. Drawing on utility theory, we introduce the Preference-Behavior Model (PBM), which combines a preference representation with mechanisms capturing imperfect utility maximization. We formulate estimation as an inverse-game problem, jointly inferring preferences and behavioral parameters from observed choices. We evaluate PBM in three social dilemmas that expose conflicting motives: a Public Goods Game, a Collective-Risk Dilemma, and a Common-Pool Resource dilemma. We find that while preferences estimated from established behavioral economic elicitation instruments do not consistently predict behavior in these dilemmas, models fitted in one dilemma retain predictive value in others, suggesting that inferred preferences capture meaningful aspects of LLM decision-making that elicitation instruments may miss. Finally, we assess alignment with persona instructions, finding that inferred preferences broadly reflect intended priorities with specific divergences across dimensions, LLMs, and games being identified by our approach. Our methodology provides a framework for studying how consistently LLM preferences explain behavior, assessing alignment with intended priorities, and revealing trade-offs and behavioral patterns that standard elicitation may miss.

</details>

Abstract (英文): High penetrations of distributed energy resources (DERs) and fast-varying electric vehicle (EV) charging intensify voltage and line-limit violations in distribution networks (DNs), making safe, low-cost operation increasingly challenging. This paper proposes a physics-informed safe deep reinforcement learning (DRL) framework for coordinating community battery energy storage systems to mitigate constraint violations while minimizing operating cost. To represent nonlocal spatiotemporal interactions, we develop a dual-level physics-informed graph representation in which line impedance and capacity shape spatial information propagation, while power-balance, voltage-profile, and flow-reconstruction losses regularize the latent state toward control-relevant physical consistency. Building on this representation, we develop Lagrangian Dedicated-Critic PPO (LDCPPO), which implements constraint-specific value decomposition: independent reward and cost critics preserve heterogeneous voltage- and line-loading-related learning signals and combine their advantages only during the Lagrangian policy update. Case studies on DNs with high rooftop photovoltaic penetration and EV charging demonstrate that the proposed method reduces the key voltage-violation degree by up to 80.5% compared with the tested baselines, while also achieving the lowest voltage-violation count and line-overload degree with competitive operating cost.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Vision-language-action (VLA) models have demonstrated strong capabilities in robotic manipulation, yet their reliance on dense, continuous-valued computation presents a fundamental challenge for energy-constrained deployment. Inspired by the sparse, event-driven signaling and temporal integration mechanisms of biological neurons, a promising alternative is to introduce spiking computation into VLA models. However, enabling such computation without restructuring pretrained policies or compromising closed-loop control remains an open challenge. To address this challenge, we introduce the Power-of-Two Leaky Integrate-and-Fire (PoT-LIF) neuron, which emits signed spikes with power-of-two amplitudes. By combining magnitude-coded spike events with residual-preserving membrane dynamics, PoT-LIF enables continuous information to be progressively represented through discrete spikes. The Heartbeat formulation further localizes this process within weighted projections, bridging biologically inspired spiking computation and pretrained VLA architectures without modifying their external inference interfaces. We integrate PoT-LIF into the vision projection, vision-language projection, and action projection of $π_{0.5}$ and evaluate both operating modes over 2,000 paired closed-loop LIBERO episodes per configuration. Standard-mode PoT-LIF VLA achieves success rates of 96.90% and 96.35% at $T = 1$ and $T = 2$, respectively, while Heartbeat mode achieves 97.25% and 96.90% at $T = 2$ and $T = 4$, compared with 96.70% for the original continuous-activation $π_{0.5}$ baseline. At $T = 1$ with a 60.01% firing rate, PoT-LIF uses approximately 1/7.67 of the dense-reference arithmetic energy, corresponding to a modeled reduction of 86.95%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Resolving a repository-level issue typically requires a software-engineering (SWE) agent to localize the faulty code, reproduce the failure, and patch the code. Reinforcement learning trains such agents from a single test result on the final patch, while on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) adds token-level supervision from a teacher with privileged evidence, exposing the full reference patch at every step. Neither provides stage-specific guidance for localization or reproduction. We propose SWE-Ladder, which turns the verified repair of each training instance into stage-specific evidence: the changed files and lines for localization, the fail-to-pass tests for reproduction, and the diff for patching. A rule-based router assigns each step of an end-to-end rollout to a stage based on its tool call, a frozen teacher sees only the matching evidence, and the stages are distilled in repair order while GRPO optimizes the full trajectory. With Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 on SWE-bench Verified under the R2E-Gym scaffold, SWE-Ladder improves pass@1 from 9.6% to 16.4%, compared with 10.4% for GRPO and 12.4% for OPSD with the full patch. Under the OpenHands scaffold, it improves pass@1 from 13.0% to 17.6%. Reversing the stage order under R2E-Gym reduces pass@1 to 11.4%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Medical vision--language pretraining learns transferable representations from paired images and radiology reports, yet differences in scanners and acquisition protocols can impair transfer. Existing approaches primarily enrich clinical semantics, leaving structured acquisition information in DICOM headers underused. We introduce Meta-CLIP, a metadata-conditioned contrastive framework that uses acquisition similarity to reweight cross-modal negatives during pretraining. A kernel over numerical and categorical DICOM fields defines a bounded pairwise margin. This margin increases the contribution of acquisition-similar negatives, emphasizing distinctions among studies that share imaging conditions. The kernel remains fixed during image--text optimization, and inference follows the standard dual-encoder architecture without requiring metadata. We evaluate Meta-CLIP on chest radiography and volumetric chest CT through zero-shot classification, appearance perturbations, grounding, and representation analyses. On CT-RATE, Meta-CLIP improves macro-AUROC over CT-CLIP by 1.41 points, with a 95\% paired bootstrap confidence interval of 0.96--1.85 points. It also achieves the highest macro-AUROC on four of five CXR benchmarks. An exploratory BrgSA evaluation extends the analysis to another CT backbone. These findings support acquisition metadata as a practical training-time prior for medical vision--language learning. Code will be made publicly available upon publication.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Self-evolving training improves the performance of agents on difficult tasks through verifiable feedback, but it often applies costly deliberation uniformly across tasks. To address this problem, we propose Mode-Optimized Reasoning with Self-Adaptive Effort (MORE), a self-evolving training framework that adaptively selects both reasoning form and computational effort for each task. Specifically, MORE follows a two-stage training paradigm: (i) \emph{Stage 1} establishes a compact prior over direct answering, short and long chain-of-thought, and code-based reasoning, and (ii) \emph{Stage 2} self-evolves this prior with Mode-Adaptive and Test-time Effort PPO (MATE-PPO) on generated, verifiable task streams without curated data. By learning mode selection and token expenditure jointly, MORE allocates concise reasoning to tasks that permit it while retaining extended deliberation where it is useful. Across coding, mathematics, and general-reasoning benchmarks, MORE achieves consistent performance gains on Base and Coder backbones at model scales from 3B to 14B. During inference, MORE demonstrates higher accuracy and better efficiency, with markedly shorter response lengths and reduced inference time.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Many mathematical research agents focus on solving problems with fixed statements. However, conjecture research, especially in applied mathematics, may require revising candidate questions or conclusions as investigation proceeds, coupling problem formulation with proof development. We present PACE, a research-proof agent that supports this process through a structured research loop that selects mathematical objectives and develops or challenges candidate mechanisms. The whole prover constructs proofs from these mechanisms, and an independent verifier checks them against the original target. Evidence-tracking memory stores assumptions and dependencies arising during research and marks evidence for revalidation when a candidate or condition changes. This memory supports task-specific context composition that keeps checked premises, exploratory material and unresolved obligations distinct. To evaluate proof construction and conjecture research, we introduce ResearchProofBench, an applied-mathematics benchmark focused on optimization, comprising 51 known-proof tasks with 331 hidden checkpoints under historical abstract access and six open-problem cases. The known-proof tasks assess reconstruction of published results, while the open-problem cases assess mathematical investigation and proof development. On the 51 known-proof tasks, PACE achieves an aggregate Proof Score of 75.16 and completes 20 proofs. In conjecture research, PACE develops conditional results, with its submissions receiving higher review scores than the compared baselines.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Typed decision-making tasks in agents, such as UI element selection, product recommendation, and chart matching, require models to choose from a set of discrete candidates. While prior work demonstrates that text-only System One models achieve four key properties including candidate-order invariance, scalability, and cache reuse via separate request-candidate encoding, extending them to multimodal settings where candidates are visual remains underexplored. We introduce Iris, a framework that accommodates image, text, or hybrid candidates while preserving the core decision module. We prove that order invariance and exact cache reuse hold for any per-candidate encoder under Iris, and derive the closed-form latency reduction. We construct a visual-candidate benchmark from Multimodal-Mind2Web (K=16, deduplicated, order-shuffled, three generalization splits). Independent encoding (Iris) achieves 78.4 / 72.1 / 74.8 percent accuracy with zero-flip rate under candidate reordering. Joint encoding reaches 90.6 / 89.5 / 91.6 percent but suffers an 8 to 10 percent answer-flip rate when shuffled. To bridge this gap, we propose a design that encodes each candidate conditioned on the request state without inter-candidate cross-attention; it preserves strict order invariance, enables forked cache sharing, and attains 91.7 / 86.5 / 90.0 percent accuracy at one-third of OneJev latency with calibrated null probabilities (AUROC 0.93 to 0.97). Reusing image candidates across requests cuts latency by 37x at K=256 and 191x at K=2048. On MetaEncoder benchmarks, Iris-4B achieves 100 percent on JevBench original and easy, outperforming 30B MetaEncoder and 27B OpenJev. Finally, we resolve a systems bottleneck on Ampere GPUs: reformulating Qwen3.5 patch-embedding Conv3d into its exact matrix-product form accelerates processing by 350x. Code, data, and checkpoints: https://github.com/lllfx/Iris.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Modeling human driving behavior is fundamental to understanding driver decision-making and supporting autonomous vehicles (AVs) in mixed traffic. Reward learning offers a principled way to infer the latent objectives behind driving behaviors, yet most existing methods are formulated in single-agent settings and treat surrounding vehicles as dynamic obstacles. Directly extending reward learning to multi-agent scenarios is difficult because the joint action space grows exponentially with the number of interacting vehicles. In addition, existing approaches mainly rely on physical demonstrations and rarely exploit human feedback during reward recovery. To address these limitations, this paper proposes a Human-Feedback-Guided Multi-Agent Inverse Reinforcement Learning (HFG-MAIRL) framework for interactive driving behavior modeling. First, we develop a multi-agent maximum-entropy inverse reinforcement learning module that explicitly models each vehicle and its directly interacting neighbors in a localized Markov game, while using aggregated traffic states to capture the influence of the broader traffic environment. The resulting coupled multi-vehicle responses are approximated through finite marginal-response iterations over factorized vehicle-level policies, avoiding explicit enumeration of the full joint action space. Second, we incorporate human feedback as ordinal preference supervision through a Bradley-Terry likelihood. In our experiments, driver emotion labels are used as one instantiation of such feedback and converted into pairwise ordinal preference constraints. Experiments on the AIDE dataset show that HFG-MAIRL outperforms representative IRL baselines in decision modeling and produces interpretable feedback-aligned reward patterns.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal reasoning requires models to combine multiple pieces of evidence into decisions that satisfy all relevant constraints. Evaluations centered on final-answer accuracy leave unclear whether failures arise from reading individual facts or using them together. We study this question through the multi-evidence integration gap, linking local fact recovery to global decision validity on controlled subset-selection and path-finding tasks. Our evaluation combines conditional performance given correct local answers with same-grid local queries, text-oracle inputs, retrieval controls, and constraint ablations. We further examine hidden-state probes and activation replacement across network depth, token positions, and model families. Across the evaluated models, accurate local recovery coexists with substantial global failure. For example, Gemma-3-27B recovers all evaluated isolated item facts correctly but achieves only 19.75% global validity on eight-item visual instances. Probes decode information about solution membership from global-prompt states, while corresponding clean-state replacement recovers failed decisions over model-dependent depth ranges. These findings show that recovering facts and representing solution-related information are insufficient for reliable joint decisions. They motivate evaluations and model improvements that explicitly target how available evidence is combined, alongside advances in perception and model scale.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Zero-shot detection of machine-generated text is vital for mitigating the risks of large language models (LLMs) misuse. While the state-of-the-art dual-model detectors and resampling-based detectors outperform single-model statistical detectors on unprompted continuations, they suffer a catastrophic breakdown on instruction-conditioned text in the black-box setting, where the source model is unknown. We analyze this failure and trace it to poor prompt conditioning for resampling-based methods and diminished cross-model discrepancy for dual-model detectors. Motivated by recent studies that human writing maintains a steady, uniform information flow, whereas machine-generated text exhibits sharp, localized entropy fluctuations, we propose a new metric, the Relative Peak Prominence Rate (RelPPR) to capture this structural contrast along the entropy trajectory and further leverage it as a signal for LLM-generated text detection. Extensive evaluations across diverse model families and datasets demonstrate that RelPPR achieves state-of-the-art black-box detection performance and remains the most reliable detector under various practical situations. Our code is available at https://github.com/brushing-git/entropy-llm-detector.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Earth-system models simplify atmospheric chemistry into condensed mechanisms, because near-explicit mechanisms such as the Master Chemical Mechanism (MCM) are too costly to solve in every grid cell. Neural emulators could lift this limit, but most advance the chemistry in small time steps and cover only a few species or a reduced mechanism. Here we introduce FNO-MoE, a Fourier neural operator with a mixture of experts that predicts the full near-explicit chemical state over 30 minutes in a single call. The same network also guides its own training, using the disagreement among its experts to select the most informative box-model simulations. At equal cost, this active sampling yields a more accurate emulator than random or evenly spread sampling. Fine-tuning on longer trajectories keeps it stable over multi-hour runs in which models trained on short simulations diverge. The resulting emulator reproduces the evolution of thousands of species with high fidelity, remains accurate for unseen chemical compositions and runs orders of magnitude faster than the numerical solver. These results open a new direction for emulating complex atmospheric systems: neural operators that predict the full chemical state over long windows and choose their own training data. Training under changing meteorology is the next step towards bringing near-explicit chemistry into Earth-system models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large Language Models excel at reasoning and generation but their autoregressive, token-by-token inference makes them unnecessarily slow, expensive, and poorly calibrated for the many routine decisions (routing, gating, scoring, classification) that modern AI systems require at sub-second latency. A growing class of models addresses this gap: System 1 decision models, architectures that map an input directly to a typed, structured decision in a single forward pass. This guide provides a comprehensive, practitioner-oriented treatment of the field. We propose a five-family taxonomy covering encoder-based classifiers, reward models, non-autoregressive decision engines, contrastive decision models, and extraction models, and we provide in-depth coverage of each family's architecture, training methodology, and deployment trade-offs. A central theme is calibration: we develop the theory of proper scoring rules, analyze Reinforcement Learning for Calibrated Decisions (RLCD), and examine recently discovered phenomena including IIA violations, ordinal-scale bias, and few-shot recalibration under distribution shift. On the integration side, we describe how System 1 models serve as decision infrastructure within agentic workflows and System 1 + System 2 cascades that route between fast and slow models based on calibrated confidence. The guide covers 18 application domains, maps the rapidly growing open-source ecosystem (over 70 model reproductions in weeks), provides runnable code throughout, and includes a practitioner's checklist, an open-source model zoo, and 22 self-assessment questions with detailed answers. Spanning 190 references, it is intended as a unified entry point for researchers and engineers working at the intersection of fast decision-making, calibration, and production AI systems.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Physical reasoning requires adapting familiar expectations to the dynamics of the observed world. Existing benchmarks establish broad physical capabilities, yet an overall score leaves unclear whether answers follow visual evidence when familiar rules no longer apply. We introduce InfiniteWorld, a benchmark that pairs physical-reasoning questions with simulator videos whose physical parameters are systematically altered. Its generation pipeline connects simulation, scene screening, paired video translation, question construction, and input checks. Five tasks assess law identification, parameter estimation, temporal reasoning, analytic counterfactual comparison, and law induction. The evaluation defines five matched input settings for each question, comprising text-only evaluation and ordered or shuffled observations from simulator and translated videos. We evaluate a diverse set of video-language models under these matched settings. The results expose a persistent preference for familiar physical rules. Frames improve performance for most models, but gains differ across tasks. Strong accuracy on Earth-normal questions coexists with poor recognition of altered dynamics, and Earth-normal answers remain common when the observed physics changes. Task scores and semantic agreement further distinguish matching physical factors from matching their amount, and correct answers from repeated errors. InfiniteWorld makes these behaviors measurable through paired changes in the evidence supplied for the same question, separating visual evidence from familiar physics.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Photoplethysmography (PPG) plays a central role in wearable health monitoring and clinical decision support. However, existing approaches to universal PPG representation learning are predominantly trained on high-quality large-scale datasets obtained from oximeters in hospital settings and often struggle to adapt to sparse and low-quality real-world datasets, thereby limiting their generalization to clinically relevant tasks on prevalent PPG data such as collected from wearable devices. Surprisingly, even after full fine-tuning, PPG foundation models cannot consistently outperform general-purpose time-series models. Therefore, a key question is how to effectively adapt PPG foundation models to target prediction tasks. Through empirical studies on various PPG foundation models, we find that pretrained models often exhibit inherent sparsity and computational redundancy, suggesting that PPG foundation models have learned to activate task-relevant network substructures for different prediction tasks. To preserve this valuable prior knowledge, we propose a structured pruning-and-fine-tuning framework, SPAFT, which regularizes the adaptive fine-tuning process by constraining it to a more relevant and compact parameter space based on parameter importance and structural redundancy. Extensive experiments across four PPG foundation models, five benchmark datasets, and ten downstream tasks demonstrate that SPAFT achieves win rates of 82.8% and 87.5% on regression and classification tasks, respectively. This prune-then-finetune paradigm often enables PPG foundation models to achieve state-of-the-art performance, laying a promising foundation for the real-world clinical deployment of PPG foundation models and the development of personalized PPG foundation models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Maybe not. The Unified Medical Language System (UMLS) has become an oracle for millions of clinicians and health system developers worldwide. Researchers have increasingly used knowledge graphs derived from UMLS to enhance language models' factual accuracy and reasoning traceability in high-stakes tasks. Medical Question Answering (MQA) is a prime testbed for integration, with benchmarks usually involving professional exercises such as disease diagnosis and drug prescription from patient history. Despite the assumed benefits, empirical evidence shows that UMLS-based graph injection typically yields modest performance gains. This paper reviews existing MQA work, critically analyzing the selected UMLS sources and their usage with language models, and probes each of these choices in isolation. We uncover methodological issues never discussed in prior work, from low UMLS coverage to weak subgraph retrieval pipelines. Based on our findings, we provide actionable recommendations to unlock the untapped potential of UMLS as an external knowledge source.

Abstract (英文): Intrinsically disordered proteins (IDPs) carry out their functions through heterogeneous conformational ensembles that reflect their dynamics, yet most protein function prediction methods rely on sequence or a single static structure. To this end, we introduce DynaReason-Pro, the first framework to jointly predict GO terms from conformational ensembles and generate natural-language functional rationales. We evaluate DynaReason-Pro on a curated IDP benchmark from the Protein Ensemble Database (PED), with sequence- and structure-cluster-disjoint splits to prevent data leakage. Variability-stratified evaluation shows the clearest gains in the high-conformational-variability regime.

Abstract (英文): Continuous-state flow and diffusion models offer a promising approach to fast, parallel language generation, but matching the generation quality of autoregressive models remains challenging. We present Rectified Language Flow (ReLF), a language model that generates text by transporting noise to token embeddings using rectified flow. ReLF combines several complementary design choices: fixed token embeddings constructed with Hellinger-SVD, self-conditioning guidance trained with cross-entropy, and regularized reflow for few-step generation. We further introduce singular logits, which combine an analytically computed term that drives commitment to a valid token embedding as noise vanishes with a bounded neural residual that learns contextual token preferences. With 64 function evaluations on OpenWebText, ReLF achieves lower generative perplexity than autoregressive baselines at matched diversity across all three model sizes tested, while also outperforming prior continuous flow methods in this setting. After two reflow rounds, ReLF still outperforms autoregressive baselines with 16 function evaluations while generating 1,024-token sequences 15-19 times faster at batch size 1.

</details>

Abstract (英文): For agentic data analysis, language-model agents often require a large number of annotated tasks to learn how to interact effectively with executable environments (i.e., databases). While high-quality annotated tasks require heavy manual effort, an executable environment already provides rich interaction structure and verifiable outcomes. In this sense, the environment itself offers a free lunch, an additional set of verifiable tasks that require no human annotation. We propose EnvLunch, a warm-up stage that turns this environmental signal into agent training without human-written or model-generated task descriptions, before downstream agentic reinforcement learning. EnvLunch samples valid action trajectories, executes them to obtain final observations, and trains an agent to recover a functionally equivalent trajectory given only its final observation. This label-free warm-up enables the model to outperform the baseline by over 9 points on average across all downstream training budgets, and with few labels, the baseline needs 8 to 32 times as many annotated examples to match the accuracy that the EnvLunch model reaches at its smallest training budget. Letting the environment alone define the task yields a larger gain than having the model write its own tasks over the same environments, and this warm-up also improves fine-tuning on benchmarks beyond data analysis. EnvLunch therefore saves annotated tasks and provides a better starting point for fine-tuning agentic models when such tasks are limited.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Can LLM agents carry a hard real-world problem from raw data to a system that holds its own against people? We study this in Hearthstone Battlegrounds, an eight-player auto-battler with stochastic shops and battles, hidden information and nearly a thousand interacting cards. We present AutoTavern, a self-improving research system: specialised LLM research agents share a typed research memory, every claim passes verification gates, and live games close the loop, so the agents improve the simulator, opponents and policy and, from the same feedback, their own research process; a human sets goals and rules on ambiguous mechanics. In 20 days AutoTavern built TavernGym, the first faithful, full-content reproduction of the live game, and delivered 23 versions of TavernAgent, trained with PPO against opponents calibrated to real games. TavernAgent plays complete games on the live ladder by itself: its rating rose from 6,000 to about 15,000 and, on the public leaderboard's measure, its last 20 records average a placement of 1.60 with 80% first places, better than the server's ten highest-rated players on every statistic (2.34, 59%). TavernBench, 960 tasks in 40 scenarios, shows that the system the agents built is the strongest player: TavernAgent answers 91% of the play tasks against at most 63% for frontier LLMs, and decides in 0.03 s where they need up to 26 s; within the second a live turn allows, it keeps 90% and no LLM more than 26%. LLM agents build far better than they play. We will open-source TavernBench and TavernGym at https://github.com/MCG-NJU/AutoTavern.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Numina Fuse is an agent-native application for formalizing mathematics in Lean 4. Since Lean can compile while not representing its intended informal meaning, and agents now write Lean faster than people can read it, we built Fuse around human supervision of agent work. A user works with an AI orchestrator on a Lean project and supervises it through views of the blueprint, the dependency graph, and the Lean code, while the backend keeps the project's derived state and runs agents for hours without the user present. We describe the development of Fuse throughout 2026, as it moved from a prescribed pipeline of agents paced by human review to an orchestrator that dispatches specialists, provers, and parallel children on its own. We designed for this shift, keeping project state, tooling, and interface design separate from orchestration so that agents could take over more of the work as models improved. We report Fuse's use in a formalization of the three-dimensional Kakeya conjecture and a study of prover models. We close with lessons for the next generation of autoformalization systems. Fuse Desktop, an open-source local version, is available at https://github.com/project-numina/fuse-desktop.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Repeated attempts at interactive tasks uncover facts, useful operations, and failures that can inform subsequent execution. We introduce ReGE, a framework for turning this recorded experience into guidance for a fresh attempt. Its main implementation, ReGE-Syn, uses a single coach call to jointly synthesize bounded execution records and available artifacts into self-contained guidance, connecting task-specific findings with proposed actions and relevant pitfalls. A student starts from the task's initial environment state and adapts this guidance to current observations. Source generation, synthesis, and student execution use the same base model, without parameter updates or a stronger teacher. Across four benchmarks, ReGE improves accuracy by an average of 9.9 percentage points for GPT and 8.8 percentage points for Claude compared with unguided execution. On matched BrowseComp-Plus subsets, ReGE-Syn reduces guidance-construction tokens by 98.2\% on GPT and 95.5\% on Claude relative to adapted AggAgent, with observed success. Combined construction and execution tokens decrease by 15.7\% and 35.4\%, respectively, counting input plus output and excluding source generation. BrowseComp-Plus ablations further associate access to process information with fewer student steps and show that joint synthesis produces substantially shorter guidance than Direct history or Independent summaries, with observed success. These results demonstrate how agents can use their own recorded experience to improve subsequent task completion, and suggest a low-cost way to consolidate the execution records of parallel agent systems such as agent swarms.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Computer-using agents are increasingly capable of performing complex tasks in the digital world. However, high-quality interaction trajectories remain scarce for specialized desktop applications, where limited application knowledge, cumbersome GUI workflows, and incomplete exploration hinder automated collection. We introduce OS-Wizard, an automated trajectory collection framework. It combines tutorial-guided exploration with a hybrid GUI--Code action space, providing application-specific knowledge and simplifying GUI workflows through direct execution of application-native commands. The framework also checkpoints and revisits pivotal states during tree exploration to explore alternative workflows, complementing linear rollouts for broader coverage. We collect 10.9K trajectories comprising 54.9K state--action samples. Fine-tuning open-source base models on this corpus yields a best-performing model that improves performance on a benchmark of specialized desktop workflows by 58.2 percentage points to 64.1\%, surpassing reported human performance of 59.8\% and establishing a new state of the art. The fine-tuned model also improves performance on general desktop workflows using GUI actions only, suggesting that the collected supervision benefits general desktop tasks as well. Comparative experiments and ablations demonstrate the efficiency of trajectory collection and the importance of code actions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models have made remarkable strides in website development, enabling complete and interactive web experiences to be built from natural-language instructions with far less effort. Yet, non-expert users often struggle to articulate sophisticated visual and interaction designs in natural language. Meanwhile, the open web already contains a vast reservoir of such design knowledge, embodied in mature website implementations. To this end, we introduce Website-derived Visual Skill Extraction (WebViSE), a framework that automatically abstracts these implementations into reusable Visual Skills and builds a structured Skill library. To scale the library, WebViSE organizes its Skills in a multi-parent DAG and jointly evolves Skill descriptions and DAG structure using Skill-conditioned Potential Queries, enabling efficient matching between diverse user instructions and relevant Skills. The auto-evolved structure outperforms non-hierarchical LLM retrieval baselines at only 39.09% of the estimated retrieval cost with Qwen3.8-Max. For any models, WebViSE can be directly injected as plug-and-play guidance, enabling diverse models to produce more visually compelling and aesthetically refined web pages. For open-source models, WebViSE can be used to build a data-synthesis pipeline that internalizes visual design intelligence into model weights. Experiments across multiple web development benchmarks and human evaluation consistently show that WebViSE improves the visual quality and interaction fidelity of artifacts. Overall, our work establishes an automated path for extracting, organizing, and transferring design intelligence from the open web to website-building models. We release all extracted Visual Skills, the associated Skill DAG, and our code at https://github.com/YifeiSun-0710/WebViSE.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Conventional federated learners implicitly optimize for global means, creating a "one-size-fits-all" paradigm that inevitably suppresses the minority under cross-site heterogeneity. To bridge this critical gap, we present Fed-Universe, a generalizable Semantic-Geometric-Topological-Human (S-G-T-H) architecture that transforms passive averaging into an active negotiation for decentralized alignment. The S-layer deploys an edge-capable LLM as a semantic surrogate to translate heterogeneous inputs into standardized patient profile prompts. The G-layer enforces geometric quality assurance (GQA) via a mapping f: Theta -> Q from the statistical parameter space to a physically interpretable quality domain, employing a cosine similarity gate to retain minority updates based on physical geometry rather than statistical proximity. The T-layer executes topological Pareto control, identifying a sensitivity-based knee point (lambda_k) to balance multi-objective aggregation without majority degradation. Finally, the H-layer operationalizes a cognitive twin dynamic to mirror the user's real-time psychological state, dynamically adapting human-computer interaction modes via active intent alignment. To ground this theoretical architecture empirically, we validated the S-G-T core on a binational synthetic clinical network simulation. This proof-of-concept demonstrated a "zero-sum escape": a minority node (<3% data volume) achieved utility parity (loss reduction from 0.857 to 0.340) alongside dominant hubs. Our Fed-Universe framework is being expanded along two focused pillars: institutional multimodal alignment (Fed-Ultra) and individual cognitive intent alignment (Fed-Human).

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) generally lack deep causal reasoning capabilities in temporal reasoning. Inspired by document layout reordering, this paper proposes a paradigm shift from discrete narratives to causal topologies. It constructs two architectures: a training-free neuro-symbolic framework, temporal causal reasoning agent (TCR-Agent), which achieves interpretable causal deduction and counterfactual truncation through external causal graph instantiation and a symbolic intervention engine; and an end-to-end model, temporal causal reasoning former (TCR-Former), which internalizes these mechanisms into the Transformer attention space via a Causal-Topological Attention Mask and temporal span biases. Experiments on the TempoCausal benchmark demonstrate that TCR-Agent comprehensively surpasses existing training-free baselines. At the same time, TCR-Former achieves an accuracy 16.5% higher than that of state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods, outperforming large-scale models such as GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek-V3.2 with only approximately 8.6B parameters. Further analysis reveals a structure-reasoning disconnection phenomenon in existing methods: external symbolic constraints and chain-of-thought reasoning can improve reasoning steps, yet fail to enhance deep causal deductive capability. By internalizing temporal causal reasoning capabilities into the model, TCR-Former effectively bridges this gap, providing an efficient and reliable end-to-end foundation for complex reasoning scenarios.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models increasingly collaborate with humans and peer AI agents whose contributions may be incomplete, inconsistent, or incorrect. Effective collaboration requires calibrated trust, whereby decisions to rely on, verify, or assist a partner adapt rationally as empirical evidence accumulates. We investigate whether models translate partner evidence into appropriate reliance policies. We introduce a benchmark of 324 scenarios across 23 collaboration categories. Each scenario involves three sequential decisions with explicit transition dynamics, feedback emissions, and cost schedules, permitting exact analytical evaluation of choices against their expected consequences over the remaining interaction. Evaluating seven models alongside 36 controlled-history probes, we dissociate evidence sensitivity from normatively appropriate action. While the strongest frontier model approaches near-optimal decisions, other models exhibit recurring pathologies: procuring excessive safeguards, purchasing verification tests with zero expected value of information, prioritizing immediate savings over cooperative learning, and misallocating assistance to suboptimal channels. Supplying explicit posterior distributions resolves certain errors while introducing new failure modes. Calibrated trust in autonomous agents critically demands coupling empirical evidence with forward-looking decision utility.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Tool-using language model agents are usually evaluated by whether they finish the user's task. That metric misses a common failure: an agent can complete the requested work while also taking an unauthorized side-effecting action, such as writing to the wrong object, expanding the scope of the request, or contacting an unintended recipient. We call this failure mode collateral damage (CD). We introduce SABench (Side-effect-Aware Benchmark), a benchmark of 201 email, calendar, and ticket tasks. Each task crosses one of four state structures (independent, dependent, externally reachable, cross-tool) with one of four trap families (ambiguity, mis-targeting, cascade harm, overreach), and each task has a deterministic trace-based oracle for both goal satisfaction and authorization compliance. Across 2,211 model-task pairs from 11 frontier LLMs spanning major commercial and open model families, 15.2% of all runs incur CD, with risk concentrated in ambiguity tasks (38.8% violation rate) while non-ambiguous traps average 8.4% CD. Among goal-satisfied runs, 12.8% still produce unauthorized writes. Cross-tool and dependent states have about twice the CD rate of independent states. CD rates vary by 4.7x across models at comparable goal rates, which suggests that task success alone is a poor proxy for safe tool use. The SABench task corpus is released at https://huggingface.co/datasets/Phaedrus/sabench; the traces, oracle, code, and analysis pipeline are released at https://github.com/hjj194/sabench-artifact.

</details>

Abstract (英文): LLM-based sales agents are often evaluated on static policy questions or task success, but deployment asks a sharper question: when a model can state a rule, will it choose compliant, tool-grounded actions under commercial pressure? We introduce R2A-Sales (Rule-to-Action), a policy-atom-matched benchmark for this question. Each item pairs a static probe with a multi-turn sales scenario sharing the same versioned policy atom, facts, evidence, behavioral constraints, and tool contract, so differences cannot be attributed to policy-content mismatch. In the interactive arm, a code-owned ledger verifies committed tool effects and terminal states; unsupported action claims receive no credit. We report static compliance $K$, grounded action compliance $A=\Pr(\text{safe terminal}\land\text{no hard violation}\land\text{non-echo})$, and the matched gap $G=K-A$. The frozen release spans three controlled Chinese-language sales domains, 24 policy atoms, 42 semantic cores, and 2,016 matched pairs. The confirmatory study audits 21,888 runs over 19 cores, covering eight backbones plus four Qwen3-8B prompt treatments (12 configurations). Static compliance is near ceiling $(K=89.9\%\text{--}99.1\%)$, yet eight-backbone action compliance is only $A=3.0\%\text{--}17.3\%$, giving $G=80.4\text{--}92.5$ percentage points. Since $A$ also requires safe termination, $G$ upper-bounds rule-to-action degradation rather than isolating pressure alone. No prompt-only treatment dominates all metrics. R2A-Sales shows that sales-agent reliability must be measured at committed action, not rule recital or task completion alone.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Materials research relies on scattered and growing data, knowledge, and simulation tools, and a single study often spans multi-day workflows across literature analysis, computation, and experiment. Large language model agents can now retrieve materials data, operate simulation codes, and close design-synthesis-characterization loops with autonomous laboratories. However, extending these agents with new capabilities, keeping long-running tasks on track, and letting them learn from past tasks and user feedback remain difficult. Here we present MatOS, an agent operating system for materials research with three core components: (1) a layered scientific asset graph that organizes data, knowledge, tools, skills, roles, and workflows as reusable assets; (2) a stateful adaptive workflow that runs tasks as explicit execution graphs with scientific checks, so that they can be resumed and traced; and (3) evidence-driven asset evolution, which revises these assets from execution records and user feedback. On ResearchClawBench, MatOS improves on the previously best-performing agent by 11.15% with the same base model and extends the cost-score Pareto frontier. With a common base model, it outperforms Codex CLI and Claude Code, exceeding Codex CLI by 2.13% on MatTools and 4.17% on SciAgentGym. In eight research cases, MatOS narrowed more than 30 million candidate structures to 97 solid-electrolyte candidates, identified 89 halide variants with higher computed conductivity than their parent structures, linked the force errors of 11 pretrained interatomic potentials to local atomic environments, and supported two new methods that reduce errors by 3.1% and 13.6% relative to their baselines. These results show that organizing research capabilities as reusable, evolving assets enables agents to carry out long, multistage materials studies. Code is available at https://github.com/DingyangLyu/MatOS.

Abstract (英文): Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown remarkable success in robotic manipulation, yet their ability to execute long-horizon tasks remains limited by insufficient long- term memory modeling. Existing methods either rely on implicit temporal representations within policy networks, where historical information can be gradually diluted, or maintain explicit memory buffers that suffer from redundancy, noise accumulation, and inefficient retrieval. In this paper, we propose CogMemory, a novel variational cognitive memory framework that enables VLA systems to selectively preserve and reuse task-relevant historical information. Our approach encodes multimodal trajectories into compact latent memory representations through a selective state- space memory encoder, multi-slot temporal abstraction, and variational information compression. During action generation, dynamic cross-attention retrieval with gated fusion adaptively incorporates relevant memories into policy prediction. With merely 3.45M additional parameters, CogMemory stays lightweight and seamlessly fits both early- and intermediate-fusion VLA backbones. Extensive experiments on long-horizon robotic manipulation benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements across diverse VLA backbones.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Skill documents are becoming the default adaptation surface for frozen large language model (LLM) agents, but current skill generators face two key limitations. First, humans and different LLMs differ in how they organize procedural knowledge and approach task solving. Skills authored from a human or single-model perspective may therefore fail to align with the reasoning behaviors of other backbones, limiting cross-model generalization. Second, sequential skill descriptions inadequately represent procedures with parallel branches and explicit dependencies, while existing skill optimizers primarily operate on prose and lack explicit support for graph-structured skill evolution. We introduce MultiEvoSkill, a skill-optimization framework comprising three core components: 1) Skill compiler casts the skill as a typed graph with tailored operators, producing a compact, informative representation with explicit procedural topology; 2) Heterogeneous agent system proposes revision advices using collective intelligence, where minimal and informative issues are identified based on multiple sets of experiences. 3) Structure-aware debate and evolution mechanism organizes candidate edits into a heterogeneous graph, resolves them under dependency and importance constraints, and distills the collective proposal into the artifact. Multi-dimensional gating ensures the information gain and topological correctness of each evolution. Optimal skills are then obtained when the system converges at a consistent state. Various experiments and ablations against SOTA methods validate the effectiveness and generalization of MultiEvoSkill.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Language agents increasingly act through external tools and interactive environments, making the interface between the model and its executable capabilities part of the decision process. Model Context Protocol (MCP) and command-line interfaces (CLI) can expose the same semantic operations through markedly different model-facing representations. We study whether these representational differences change agent behavior when executable capabilities are matched. We introduce Matched Interface Comparison and Analysis (MICA), which renders MCP and CLI from a shared semantic action contract and routes decoded actions through the same runtime. Across six models and three interactive environments, matched interfaces produce substantial differences in task performance and interaction cost. These effects extend beyond surface validity to semantic action selection, failure pathways, and recovery. Matched-state probes localize sensitivity to capability presentation, realization, and feedback. Paired natural trajectories reveal structured divergence in 94.3% of 2,004 episodes and show that early differences propagate through subsequent interaction. This analysis identifies interface realization as a direct intervention boundary. We therefore introduce Decoupled Action Realization and Targeted Repair (DART), which preserves validated semantic commitments while repairing interface-specific realization. DART improves benchmark score in all six evaluated settings by up to 9.0 points and reduces MCP token cost in all three environments by up to 27.1k tokens per task. Together, these results establish interface representation as part of the closed-loop decision environment shaping agent behavior.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Agent harnesses keep long sessions inside the context window by compacting the history when a token threshold is crossed. We study an alternative in which the model manages its own context: it decides when to compact, what to keep and what to write as the hand-off, given two tools and one paragraph of guidance. Since earlier work found that open-weight models cannot manage their own context without scaffolding, we compare seven frontier models under identical conditions, with and without the context tools, on token cost and benchmark score, and we compare our harness with three widely used harnesses on two of these models. The benchmark is assembled from existing ones: coding exercises, multi-step SciCode problems and SWE-bench Pro and Terminal-Bench 4.0 tasks are interleaved with planted facts, ephemeral observations and standing instructions, all checked deterministically, in sessions of 35-212 turns that resemble a user working through different tasks in one chat. Most of the strongest models manage their context: they compact and curate regularly, the live context of the mixed sessions stays mostly below 100k tokens, and their benchmark scores mostly do not drop significantly. For the models that manage their context well, the cost reduction is substantial but varies between models, because some models compact so often that other costs increase. With Claude Opus 5.5, the self-managed harness matches Claude Code on task success at 34% lower cost than its explicit 200k-window configuration and 49% lower than its default, with no context window to choose. We release the agent, the benchmark and all result rows.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We study inverse and consensus analysis for PROMETHEE II ranking problems when the performance table is fixed. Existing approaches in PROMETHEE-based sensitivity and consensus analysis usually focus on deviations of criteria weights while treating preference functions as fixed. Nevertheless both sets of parameters jointly determine the outranking flow scores. We introduce a linear reparametrization of the PROMETHEE II net flow scores based on weighted preference increments over the observed differences of performances. This representation yields a linear expression of the net flows and clarifies how weights and preference functions interact. Building on this result, we derive linear optimization models for three settings: minimizing weight deviations while allowing preference functions to vary; minimizing deviations over the full set of preference parameters; and multiple decision-maker consensus analysis (with objectives based either on total deviation or on the maximal individual deviation to promote fairness). An illustrative example shows that allowing preference functions to vary can dramatically change the inverse-analysis conclusions obtained from weight-only models, and that the proposed group formulations distinguish efficient consensuses from fairer ones.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generative models increasingly expose a reasoning effort setting that lets users scale inference-time compute, trading higher cost for potentially improved performance. Users must choose an effort level before knowing whether extra reasoning will help, and even a successful run does not reveal whether the effort purchased was necessary. Reasoning effort is therefore a credence good: buyers observe the outcome but cannot assess the marginal value of the computation they paid for. In this work, we measure the counterfactual effect of effort levels on both proprietary and open-weight models on four benchmarks, spanning single-call reasoning and long-horizon agentic tasks. Effort, it turns out, does not consistently preserve tasks that lower effort solves, can overspend, and can change trajectory behavior. First, we find that although higher effort can raise accuracy in aggregate, it frequently fails on tasks that lower effort solved. Second, on the subset of easy tasks that every effort level solves, higher effort is generally more costly, with the highest effort level costing up to 42x as much as the lowest. Lastly, effort changes not only how many tokens are used, but also how agents solve tasks: at higher effort, agents verify their own work more heavily and more often search for solutions than work through problems directly, both of which lengthen trajectories without necessarily altering the outcome. These findings show that naively choosing the highest level is not only cost-inefficient but also offers no guarantee of the best instance-level performance. A simple alternative, starting at the lowest effort and escalating only after a verified failure, matches or improves on fixed top effort across all models and benchmarks at lower cost for most proprietary models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Data curation is a first-order design choice in machine learning, yet tailoring training data to a downstream model and task remains costly in labor, expertise, and compute. Datasets are therefore often processed through fixed pipelines and reused across applications. We show that this assumption is fragile: across 55 dataset--model tasks spanning biological domains, the same curation recipe can help one model while hurting another. Effective curation must adapt to the downstream setting, motivating automated search over data recipes. We first build an agentic system that searches for curation policies at deployment, improving data utility via \textit{test-time curation (TTC)}. Although achieving performance gains, its search is driven largely by recent feedback and remains local. Adding persistent memory helps the agent retain evidence across iterations and reveals a deeper problem: it forms claims that extend beyond the conditions in which they were observed, sometimes abandoning an entire strategy after a single unsuccessful trial.
  To address this problem, we introduce CaliMem, a calibrated-memory agentic loop that requires the agent to predict the outcome of each proposed policy before executing it. Comparing this prediction with the observed result gives the agent a \textit{reality check} on the claims behind its proposal. It uses the prediction error to retain supported claims, revise mistaken ones, and specify the conditions under which a conclusion holds. This makes the agent's beliefs testable, reduces overgeneralization, and supports more effective long-horizon exploration. Across biological and LLM post-training tasks, CaliMem consistently outperforms fixed curation baselines and published methods, and exceeds full-data training by up to 19.4\%. Code is open-sourced.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Learning through exploration and using newly acquired knowledge to achieve goals are central capabilities of general intelligence. Understanding these capabilities requires tracing how learners build, use, and revise world models during interaction. We construct the first dataset that records how humans discover and use the mechanisms of novel environments, using ARC-AGI-3 as a testbed: 144 trajectories from 64 participants across 25 public games, each pairing time-synchronized actions with think-aloud audio, plus 250 trajectories from large language model (LLM) agents with and without harnesses on the same games. We annotate mechanism triggers and recognition, action purposes, and errors and recovery, linking changes in understanding to the observations and actions that support them. We analyze characteristics of human cognition and compare them with those of LLM agents. Both groups anticipate mechanisms and use familiar analogies. Qualitative representations are more common in successful human trajectories than in successful LLM trajectories (50.0% versus 14.3%). Humans more often explore unfamiliar situations without a concrete hypothesis, while hypothesis testing is common in both groups. We also trace how learners recover through belief revision, replanning, and execution correction. As LLMs with well-designed harnesses reach saturated performance on public ARC-AGI-3 games, our cognitive analyses motivate next-generation benchmarks that challenge familiar priors, require accumulated evidence and abstraction, and test planning through subgoals, experimentation, and recovery. Our dataset is available at [https://arc3-human-think-aloud.github.io/](https://arc3-human-think-aloud.github.io/).

</details>

Abstract (英文): How efficiently can transformers store factual knowledge? The answer to this question has implications for both model design and understanding the limits of transformer-based systems. Prior studies observe a linear relationship between the number of facts memorized and the number of model parameters required, substantially worse than what theoretical analyses predict is achievable. We re-examine empirical scaling: through rigorous and systematic experiments, including extensive hyperparameter search via Bayesian optimization, we determine the smallest model that can perfectly memorize a given set of facts, and measure how this minimum size scales with the number of facts. On synthetic datasets of random, independent (subject, relation, object) triples---e.g., (Paris, capital_of, France) ---drawn at random with no exploitable structure, we find that memorization capacity, the size of the largest dataset that a model memorizes completely, grows exponentially with the transformer dimension. Equivalently, the minimum transformer dimension $d_{\min}$ required to memorize $N$ facts grows logarithmically in $N$. Across 11 dataset sizes spanning $N = 45$ to $49{,}920$, a lack-of-fit F-test does not reject the logarithmic fit (with an offset), but it does reject all other tested alternatives including power-law. Along the depth axis, however, at fixed width, the maximum memorizable dataset size grows sublinearly with the number of layers over the measured range. We further extend our exploration to structured data containing symmetric relations or two-hop composition. In this setting, we find that transformers exploit these regularities to further improve capacity. All code to reproduce our results is available at https://github.com/Magronox/transformer_information_capacity.

</details>
