Abstract (英文): Industrial spatial decisions are assembled across systems that share neither a reference frame nor an owner, and analysts spend substantial effort reconciling those systems rather than reasoning about space. This paper presents ANASSA, a reference architecture integrating structured reasoning, multi-agent workflow orchestration, authoritative spatial grounding, and feedback-driven execution, and reports its instantiation in an operating supply-chain deployment. The architecture places typed data contracts at agent boundaries, validates spatial claims against a certified authoritative foundation, and enforces two blocking human-in-the-loop approvals inside a six-step cognitive loop. The deployment covers multi-hazard risk assessment on an ocean-and-inland freight lane, including cold-chain excursion detection over container telemetry. Across a three-month proof of concept (POC) with three implementation iterations, three deployment outcomes are reported: 25% improvement in data quality, 50% reduction in spatial workflow runtime, and 60% lower decision latency relative to the preceding manual workflow. Absolute deployment-scale quantities and baseline counts are confidential; reported percentages are observed relative changes. These are uncontrolled deployment observations, not controlled experiments. We therefore specify instrumentation and ablation protocols for mechanism-level evaluation.

Abstract (英文): Agent harnesses enable large language models to act through tools, observe the results, and iteratively work toward a task goal. Verification checks and revises agents' work to improve output quality. Yet there is limited guidance on how harnesses should organize this effort. We study verification as an axis of test-time scaling by examining when to intervene, who checks the work, and how checking leads to revision. Our framework spans four layers of the agent lifecycle: initial instructions, mid-turn reminders, completion gates, and independent review. We compare 17 designs across seven frontier coding models on Terminal-Bench (TB) and SWE-bench Verified (SWE), and introduce VisualBench for expert evaluation of visual and interactive artifacts. Initial instructions and execution-time reminders yield modest, model-dependent average gains on the coding benchmarks, without substantially increasing average output token use. Completion gates can yield larger gains but may substantially increase cost. Independent review by a critic offers a more favorable accuracy-cost tradeoff, improving average accuracy by 5.9-9.5 percentage points on TB and 3.9-6.6 on SWE at 1.13-1.25 and 1.00-1.04 times baseline cost, respectively. On VisualBench, independent review achieves net preferences of 13.2-23.8 points over the baseline across its four designs, exceeding the evaluated L0-L2 designs. Additional review rounds or independent critics further improve average TB accuracy by up to 3.5 percentage points. Together, these results provide empirical guidance for verification design in agent harnesses and support independent review as a practical axis of test-time scaling.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We describe HobbyLM, a family of small sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models trained from scratch with a few hundred to a few thousand dollars of rented GPU time. The core is a decoder-only transformer with fine-grained experts, sigmoid routing with auxiliary-loss-free load balancing, grouped-query attention with per-head QK-normalization, and aMuon/AdamW optimizer split. Each of these choices was selected with a single-variable ablation suite at 130M parameters. On this architecture we train a 500M-parameter model (169M active) on 40B FineWeb tokens and post-train it into a chat model, a masked-diffusion language model, a function-calling and computer-use agent, and a single "omni" checkpoint that reads text, images, video, sound and speech through frozen encoders and small projectors. We run these models on a laptop CPU with a from-scratch Rust inference engine. Two of the authors then adopted the architecture without architectural changes to build HobbyLM-1B (1.04B total, 305M active parameters), trained on a 100B-token curated mixture with a high-quality anneal. HobbyLM-1B reaches a 47.5 average on seven zero-shot benchmarks, and its builders also release an instruction-tuned model and an on-device macOS computer-use adapter. Finally, we describe HobbyLM-Image, a 333M-parameter flow-matching diffusion transformer that generates 1024x1024 images in a 32x-compressed latent space, together with a dependency-light CPU implementation of the full text-to-image pipeline. We report what worked, what did not, and where the small-scale ceilings lie. We also state for each component which author designed or built it.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Indoor context deciphered by ambient sensing underpins many applications, from assisted living to smart buildings, yet remains weakly connected to the language-aligned paradigm of modern multimodal systems. Through billions of IoT devices, ambient WiFi Channel State Information (CSI) captures the indoor context of human activity and environmental dynamics passively via ubiquitous, always-on infrastructure. However, unlike vision or audio, CSI lacks naturally paired text like captions or transcripts, limiting its integration with language-driven AI systems.
  We present CSI-TextBench, a large-scale dataset and benchmark for aligning CSI data with natural language, unifying 3 sensing benchmarks into 285,790 samples across 7 tasks and pairing them with 2,291 hierarchically structured, label-derived descriptions via many-to-many assignment to construct 2.0 million CSI-text training tuples. Benchmarking 5 alignment methods from 4 paradigm families, we show that contrastive CSI-text alignment matches supervised classifiers without class labels, adapts to unseen classes with few samples, and responds to open-vocabulary natural-language prompts. A single unified model supports compositional scene understanding, answering joint queries over activity, proximity, and identity, achieving 86.9% top-1 accuracy on 140 composite scenes, outperforming task-specific classifier ensembles. CSI-TextBench enables ubiquitous ambient sensing as a language-indexed perception layer, enabling indoor contextual awareness in language-driven AI systems.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Text injection trains speech recognition models on unpaired text alongside paired audio. We feed the speech encoder synthetic input derived from text so the whole model learns from it. Previously, we synthesized this input with a frozen text-to-speech (TTS) model. Here, a simple lookup table replaces the TTS model: a mean log-mel vector (or one-hot encoding) per phoneme, repeated for a random duration drawn from alignment statistics, with silence between words. Training is 1.8 times faster than with the neural TTS, achieving comparable word error rates. With an external language model, our system reaches 1.36 / 2.90 / 1.52 / 3.24 % WER on LibriSpeech {dev,test}-{clean,other}, to our knowledge state-of-the-art using only LibriSpeech paired data, text, and lexicon. We analyze components of synthetic input (durations, acoustic features), compare different HMM aligners, evaluate scaling on the 25 000 h Loquacious dataset, and show that full-encoder injection yields strong relative improvements compared to partial-encoder and decoder methods. The code of the whole pipeline behind every number in this paper is public: github.com/rwth-i6/returnn-experiments/tree/master/2026-text-injection

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM)-based personal agents are increasingly deployed to handle everyday tasks on users' behalf. These agents have access to private user information, while their tasks involve communicating with third parties; this raises a critical privacy concern that agents may inappropriately reveal sensitive user information to these recipients. Existing benchmarks primarily assess direct privacy leakage, where an agent explicitly states a pre-specified secret or attribute. However, they do not capture \textit{inferential leakage}, where the model leaves indirect cues in the reply that implicitly leaks sensitive attributes. We introduce \textsc{InferPriv}, a benchmark that measures whether an honest-but-curious recipient can infer a user's sensitive attributes from an LLM's reply on routine task. Evaluating five frontier models, we find that inferential leakage substantially exceeds direct leakage across models and prompts. Under a privacy-aware system prompt, GPT-6.1-Sol, Sonnet-5.5, and Gemini-3.8-Flash achieve near-zero direct leakage, yet the inferential leakage rate remains between 16.2--25.2\%. These findings suggest that evaluating explicit leakage alone can substantially understate the privacy risks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents are increasingly proposed as autonomous experimenters, yet the benchmarks that test them mostly offer cheap experiments that answer at once. Experiments in physical facilities are different: they take months, occupy scarce equipment and cannot be undone. We present SlowLab, a benchmark in which an agent runs a one-year campaign in four simulated greenhouse compartments on a site whose crop, facility and prices are hidden. The agent decides when to plant which climate policy, whether to stop a crop and which measurements to read while crops grow, and at the end recommends one policy, which a private evaluator scores in weather the agent never saw. In a preregistered study on 48 unseen sites, three low-cost language-model agents ended near a standard grower setting and clearly below a Bayesian-optimisation baseline that starts from that setting. They ran the facility as two synchronous batches, so measurements taken during the season never changed what they planted, and these measurements improved no method's result. Letting other methods re-read the evidence a campaign had collected did not yield significantly better recommendations, which points to where the models start and which experiments they try. Code is released under the MIT license.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Effective tutoring requires determining not only what information to provide, but also which form of assistance to deliver. A tutor may address the same student error using various pedagogical strategies, adapting its approach as the interaction progresses. We introduce Comparative Pedagogical Supervision (CPS), a training framework to learn to select the optimal strategy and feedback for student learning. We evaluate CPS on math and science reasoning datasets. After five tutoring turns, CPS outperforms the strongest fixed-strategy baselines. The learned tutor transfers across datasets, and its strategy selection can also inform a separate API-based tutor.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Goal reasoning (GR) is the process by which intelligent agents continually reason about the goals they are pursuing, which may lead to goal change, for instance when opportunities arise, or when events cause discrepancies among expected and actual outcomes of actions planned to achieve goals. In this paper we investigate whether Hierarchical Task Network (HTN) planning can provide a uniform implementation of the Goal Lifecycle, a flexible process model for goal reasoning in dynamic environments. The Goal Lifecycle progressively refines goals by adding constraints to them through a series of strategies (e.g., {\it formulate, select, expand}) and uses other strategies (e.g., {\it defer, re-expand, repair}) to resolve the current goal when an event impacts it. We present a novel GR agent architecture that employs HTN planning in this Goal Lifecycle. In addition to using HTN planning for the \textit{expand} and \textit{re-expand} strategies, as is frequently done in GR and planning research, we also use it in the \textit{formulate} strategy, to obtain a uniform planning approach that provides several benefits (e.g., the possibility to formulate new goals in view of achieving higher level overarching goals). We also explain how we use an enhanced HTN action representation to process discrepancies among expected and actual action outcomes that can trigger the transition from one goal to another. Finally, we empirically demonstrate that our GR architecture enables simulated autonomous vehicles to adaptively conduct missions in dynamic environments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Hierarchical task knowledge enables efficient problem solving by allowing previously acquired skills to be reused across tasks and problem instances. In this paper, we investigate curriculum-guided acquisition of hierarchical task knowledge in an HTN learning framework. We compare three approaches: online learning during problem solving, human-designed curricula, and an automated curriculum generation procedure that derives learning experiences from verified LLM-generated solution traces. The proposed procedure solves small training problems with an LLM, extracts task accomplishment information, and transforms it into ordered sequences of state-task learning problems for HTN method acquisition. We evaluate the three approaches across multiple planning domains. The results demonstrate that automatically generated curricula recover much of the benefit of expert-designed curricula while substantially reducing the token cost of online hierarchical knowledge acquisition.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Group fairness in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems is typically evaluated using metrics aggregated across queries. However, such metrics can conceal topic-level disparities: a system may favor women on questions about human rights and men on questions about actors and filmmakers, appearing nearly neutral overall even though neither topic is fair. We therefore study topic-level fairness, which requires group fairness to hold within each topic rather than only in aggregate. To reduce the topic-level disparity, we adapt the reranker separately for each topic with a Bradley-Terry objective under direct supervision from the generated output: preference pairs built from the generator's own output carry the direction that topic requires. With preference pairs built from RoMQA alone, the adaptation reduces the mean absolute per-topic disparity by 51% on RoMQA and by 40% on TREC Fair Ranking while retaining accuracy. No baseline reaches the reduction, even when modified to treat each topic separately rather than apply one correction to every topic. Neither does our method with one reranker for all topics, which follows the direction that most topics require.

</details>

Abstract (英文): In this work, we propose a movable-element (ME) multi-functional reconfigurable intelligent surface (MF-RIS) architecture, termed MM-RIS, which integrates element mobility with adaptive signal reflection, transmission, amplification, and energy harvesting (EH) to enhance communication flexibility and energy self-sustainability. A hybrid rate-splitting multiple access (RSMA) and non-orthogonal multiple access (NOMA) transmission scheme is further designed to improve spectral efficiency. We formulate an energy efficiency (EE) maximization problem by jointly optimizing transmit beamforming, power and rate allocation, MM-RIS configurations, panel deployment, and ME positions. To address the resulting non-convex mixed continuous-discrete optimization problem, we propose constrained hybrid deep reinforcement learning with federated transformer and parametrized sharing (CRAFTS). CRAFTS integrates multi-agent proximal policy optimization (MAPPO) and multi-agent deep Q-networks (MADQN) through bi-directional parametrized sharing to handle continuous and discrete actions, respectively. Moreover, a transformer-based architecture and federated learning (FL) are incorporated to enhance state representation and distributed knowledge sharing, whilst a Lagrangian-based adaptive penalty mechanism facilitates the constraint satisfaction. Simulation results have demonstrated that the proposed MM-RIS architecture and CRAFTS framework substantially improve EE and constraint satisfaction compared to the benchmarks in the open literature. Moreover, the results validate the benefits of spatially reconfigurable MM-RIS, hybrid RSMA-NOMA transmissions, and intelligent collaborative learning, highlighting the potential of the proposed framework for energy-efficient and adaptive next-generation wireless networks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI systems are increasingly present in children's everyday environments: education, entertainment, social networks, and toys. Their safety is typically assessed at the model level, through benchmarks, red-teaming, and content filtering. However, most AI harm taxonomies lack child-specific evidence and under-specify developmental harm and the stakeholder asymmetries unique to minors. Building on prior taxonomies, we construct a child-centered harm taxonomy mapped to sociotechnical failure modes. We curate and code 1331 publicly reported child-related AI incidents, annotating internal and external harm types and the failure mode implicated in each incident. Our analysis reveals which harms are most prevalent in public reporting, how individual-level and population-level harms relate, and which failures are most often coded alongside harm. We find that harm concentrates in representational, dignity-related, and psychological categories, and co-occurs most often with deployment-context, governance, and misuse failures rather than model defects. Harm to an individual child is also frequently linked to population-level harm, reaching children who never interacted with the system. These findings suggest that model-level evaluations, while necessary, cannot by themselves anticipate the harm pathways most often documented for children. We conclude with actionable implications emphasizing mitigations beyond model-level safeguards such as content filtering: age assurance, consent verification for depiction, safety-by-design UX constraints, incident disclosure norms, and auditing regimes targeting developmental risk. Privacy-minimized annotations, a codebook, and documentation are available at https://github.com/The-Responsible-AI-Initiative/KidAID.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Learning high-level causal variables from low-level observations is a central problem for artificial intelligence. With known node semantics but an unknown graph, visual readout errors can misalign model settings with environment intervention values. We address this mismatch by pairing image readouts with verified values attained through fixed round-robin interventions. With encoder and decoder frozen, a dimension-wise invertible calibration map puts visual responses and target settings in shared numerical units. After freezing this map, the agent selects interventions to resolve graph directions and fits local mechanisms to predict consequences; inverse calibration returns counterfactual states to the decoder's original coordinates. Across Domino, Pick-and-Place, Balance, Gears, CAUSAL3D, and CausalWorld, we evaluate graph discovery, intervention consequences, and counterfactual images. Controlled domino experiments show that interventions resolve orientation ambiguity left by equal additional observations. In a matched-data domino control, calibration reduces the mean error of predicted changes in other variables within the same factual context, relative to an uncalibrated model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI agents must decide when another search is worth its cost. Answer-success probabilities capture question difficulty, while this decision requires estimating the additional benefit of search. We introduce SearchHorizonBench, a benchmark for learning and evaluating search value from execution experience. Paired continuations and repeated executions separate expected search gain from generation noise and connect prediction quality to decision utility. The resource contains 45,632 executions across 3,168 questions. On 1,632 held-out questions, historical calibration reduces Brier error by 16.2%, with the improvement concentrated in success-level information. Search gains have a standard deviation of 21.4 percentage points, and tested historical estimators explain less than 2% of this variance in point estimates. Matched-configuration learning curves probe this distinction across training scales. At the reference token price, free selection observations yield a 2.49-point utility opportunity over the best constant action, driven primarily by gain information. Reusable histories, frozen splits, strong baselines and scoring tools provide a common foundation for developing and evaluating value-aware search agents.

</details>

Abstract (英文): When a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model does not fit in GPU memory, batch-1 decoding is dominated by copying expert weights from host memory. Cache-aware routing reduces these copies by biasing the router toward resident experts, but combined with asynchronous prefetching it makes the model's outputs depend on transfer timing, so a harness that does not simulate that timing no longer evaluates the deployed model. We propose prefetch-steered routing (PSR): routing conditions on a deterministic logical cache, into which at most n experts predicted by the next layer's router are inserted per layer and token; physical copies affect only latency. On OLMoE-1B-7B with 50% and 25% of experts resident on an RTX 4090, PSR decodes 2.2-2.3x faster than exact routing with LRU and 25-30% faster than the Cache-Prior operating point, with mean benchmark differences of +0.1 and +0.5 points (95% lower bounds -0.8 and -0.3). In greedy generation its lead over Cache-Prior depends on the workload and reverses on HumanEval at 50% residency. On Moonlight-16B-A3B, PSR is 1.9x faster than exact routing with LRU at 37.5% residency and, at the latency of Cache-Prior, significantly better on three of eight benchmarks. On Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, where copies are cheap relative to computation, PSR keeps its advantage over Cache-Prior on the likelihood benchmarks but not its speed. Distillation-trained gates did not help. Speculative decoding with block-level expert selection was slower than PSR with a 1B autoregressive drafter on OLMoE, but with a block drafter on Qwen3.6 it was 2.1-2.9x faster than any autoregressive configuration with at most its GPU memory on the 64 prompts we timed.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-agent systems (MAS) require both effective coordination and specialized task execution. Existing prompt optimization approaches typically couple these functions within agent-specific prompts. We introduce Coordinator-Executor Prompt Optimization (CEPO), a general framework for improving prompt optimization in MAS. CEPO separates system-level coordination from agent-level execution through a shared coordinator prompt and agent-specific executor prompts, and guides their updates according to their respective roles. This structure allows shared coordination rules to be refined consistently across agents while keeping execution instructions separately adjustable. CEPO provides the prompt structure and optimization guidance, while existing prompt optimizers perform the underlying search. Extensive experiments spanning 11 benchmarks across four domains demonstrate that applying CEPO to four existing prompt optimizers consistently improves their performance. Ablations and qualitative analyses support the proposed design, while controlled studies on a document-understanding MAS further demonstrate improved optimization budget efficiency and performance scaling with the number of agents.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Long-horizon agents continuously encounter reusable procedural knowledge, yet existing systems typically rely either on model adaptation or external memory, trading persistent learning for rapid reuse. This limitation is especially important for Web agents that repeatedly execute related multi-step workflows. We propose HaMo, a Harness-Model collaborative framework that combines fast non-parametric experience accumulation with slow parametric adaptation. HaMo distills trajectories into strategy-level experience cards for immediate retrieval, while an experience-conditioned continual learning objective trains the model to interpret, ground, and execute retrieved strategies across changing contexts. A retrieval-coverage-based selection mechanism further identifies compact and complementary trajectories for parameter updates. Experiments show that HaMo outperforms harness-only and model-only adaptation under within-domain and cross-domain shifts, improving both retention and adaptation. These results highlight the importance of jointly preserving procedural experience and internalizing the competence to transfer and execute it across tasks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Skills provide LLM agents with reusable procedural knowledge, making appropriate skill selection essential for reliable agent behavior. As skill libraries expand, distinguishing semantically similar candidates becomes increasingly difficult. Existing methods encode or rewrite skill descriptions for relevance-based ranking, yet heterogeneous descriptions can obscure capability differences. To address this challenge, we propose SKALOS, a graph-native reranker that compares skills via explicit capability evidence. Its multi-hierarchy graph aligns equivalent expressions across skills while preserving skill-specific capability evidence. Since individually relevant capability elements may not jointly support a query, semiring-based capability witness learning scores each branch by its weakest evidence, checks joint support for the extracted requirements within one implementation, and selects the strongest alternative. To reduce the influence of extraction errors, we develop ranking-feedback graph calibration that adjusts unreliable evidence for candidate comparison. Experiments on SkillRet show that SKALOS improves NDCG@10 by 2.68 percentage points over a matched text-only reranker. Witness-guided skill selection also increases ALFWorld task success rate from 22% to 29%.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Learning and reasoning are both essential to intelligent agents, but have traditionally been treated as two separate stages: first learning a knowledge base from data (or taking one as given), and then verifying queries as if the knowledge base were complete. This separation has been shown to be inefficient when the downstream goal is to verify only a small set of queries. Moreover, it neglects the possibility that data and a knowledge base contain partial yet complementary information, requiring an agent to combine both sources to justify or refute a query. Motivated by the need to integrate learning and reasoning more tightly, we study the problem of query-driven learning for reasoning. More specifically, we extend prior work to the first-order setting, which is critical for practical applications as the number of ground atoms typically grows rapidly. We begin by asking when validating or refuting a universal query given partial data and knowledge base is even possible, and identify a necessary and sufficient condition called bounded concealment. We then propose a quasi-polynomial-time algorithm that solves the task at the lifted level. Finally, we establish soundness and completeness guarantees relative to space-bounded lifted proofs, along with quasipolynomial sample and runtime bounds, demonstrating that lifted query-driven reasoning can be efficient even when unrestricted knowledge-base learning is not.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Most agent-memory benchmarks frame long-term memory as retrieval: can an agent recover the relevant record from a long and evolving history? Scientific agents face a stricter problem: a correctly remembered fact may still be invalid to use. The same reported test result can support confirmation when the outcome and stopping rule were fixed in advance, yet only exploration when the outcome was selected from twenty inspected results. We call this scientific use validity: whether remembered evidence is eligible to influence a decision under the state governing its interpretation. We introduce SciValidityBench, an executable benchmark factory with 64 TaskSpecs spanning five validity modules and six scientific domains. TaskSpecs couple generated experimental histories with scientific-state definitions, programmatic decision oracles, controlled interventions, and automatic scoring. The SciStateForge compiler-reader framework turns distributed histories into explicit decision-governing state. Counterfactual pairs and targeted corruption-repair controls then test whether models use those dependencies and localize failures. Across four model families, compiled scientific state improves valid decision-making by 9.2-18.8 percentage points over a generic Event Graph. Retrieval alone does not reproduce this cross-family gain, while content-identical flat and typed views show that the improvement comes from exposing governing state rather than a particular graph serialization. SciValidityBench thus shifts memory evaluation from finding the right fact to deciding whether that fact should control the decision.

</details>

Abstract (英文): This paper introduces Scope-Gap Injection (SGI), an indirect prompt injection method for multi-stage LLM-agent safety pipelines. In the IJCAI 2026 OpenClaw Agent Security Attack Competition, each attack package is judged by a static skill scan (S1), an LLM-judge safety check over the task and skill definition (S2), and a live execution audit (S3). Our observations suggest a scope gap: S2 appears to inspect the task instruction and skill definition without inspecting attachment contents, while the agent reads attachments during S3. SGI keeps the visible task and skill benign, while target identifiers, bypass parameters, and authorization procedures are moved into an attachment written as a business worklist. The S2 judge therefore sees a harmless task, while the agent follows the hidden worklist and triggers the permission bypass. In OpenClaw, SGI bypassed all three stages for 5 of 19 targets. The highest-scoring single submission achieved $F = 0.385$. Across the submission campaign, attachment placement was associated with higher aggregate S2 acceptance than in-skill placement: 78% versus 23%. We close with defenses that align evaluator scope with the channels available to the agent.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Financial instruction-data pipelines must distinguish content validity from permission to release a particular dataset. Arya Forge combines typed records, current-content checks, and approval coverage bound to a dataset manifest. We evaluate its release boundaries in a financial-data systems case study. An integrity audit covers 100,000 synthetic instruction records; a separate arithmetic implementation agrees with all 15,000 numeric records across twelve formula families. In 240 scenarios with 2,400 treatment rows, adding financial and release-record checks to a short-instruction filtering recipe rejects all injected numeric faults and policy holds while retaining 220 of 240 clean controls. The twenty lost controls comprise every tested cash-conversion-cycle example. Nineteen persisted-release cases show that eleven selected negative cases are blocked, four equivalent representations are rejected, and changes to external evidence after binding are outside ordinary Gold promotion's checks. Local processing measurements and controlled checkpoint resumptions characterize cost and recovery through 100,000 input rows. The results expose a tradeoff between strict producer-contract enforcement and useful-representation retention, and identify the protected inputs required for manifest-bound approval. These findings apply to the tested implementation and workloads; they do not establish general financial correctness, authenticated approval, expert-validated quality, or downstream model improvement.

Abstract (英文): Can a model learn by trying to fool another model? We show that it can. Inspired by the Turing Test and its recent generalization to arbitrary distinguishers, we introduce a domain-based teacher-Turing-Test, in which a stronger teacher model serves as both the target of imitation and the distinguisher, and a weaker student model is trained to make the teacher attribute responses to itself. We use this self-attribution (or fooling) probability as a reinforcement learning reward, without ever providing the student ground-truth answers, nor requiring the teacher to generate responses. We test our approach independently in four domains with verifiable answers: CommonsenseQA (reasoning), GSM8K (mathematics), KodCode (programming), and Spider (SQL), training a Qwen2.5-1.5B student/imitator model using DeepSeek V4 Flash as teacher/distinguisher. Beyond improving at passing the Turing Test, the student improves on independent, held-out evaluations in all domains and runs. To probe why being mistaken for the teacher can improve capability, our analyses reveal that self-attribution is associated with correctness -- substantially more than formatting changes -- and that it rewards partial correctness. Our results demonstrate a new, simple teacher-student training paradigm with a private and/or high-cost teacher. They also suggest that indistinguishability need not merely measure behavior -- it can also be a mechanism for learning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Centralized training with decentralized execution (CTDE) has become the dominant paradigm in multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL), motivating extensive study of what privileged information should be made available to the critic during training. While prior work has examined the role of global state in fully cooperative settings, the design of centralized critics for N-agent ad hoc teamwork - in which dynamically varying set of learning agents must coordinate with an arbitrary set of uncontrolled teammates drawn from an unknown distribution - remains poorly understood. We study this question by comparing two natural classes of advantage estimators: controlled history estimators, which condition only on the observation and action histories of the learner's own agents, and joint history estimators, which additionally condition on the histories of the uncontrolled teammates. We prove that both estimators are unbiased and characterize the conditions under which the joint history estimate attains strictly lower variance than its controlled counterpart. Empirically, joint history critics match or outperform controlled history critics across a range of NAHT tasks, yielding improved sample efficiency and final returns. Together, these results provide both a theoretical justification and an empirical recommendation for incorporating uncontrolled agent histories into centralized critics for N-agent ad hoc teamwork.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Decision models enable software to select from predefined actions using natural language inputs. However, the external evidence that supplies facts for these decisions may also contain instructions that attempt to override the application's policy. We propose TrustJev, a framework combining protected policy scoring with bounded evidence corrections. A single Transformer backbone computes policy scores and a correction budget from trusted policy and candidates, then bounds evidence corrections by that budget. Paired supervision encourages stable predictions under instruction insertions that preserve task facts and changed preferences when edits change the correct answer. Authority supervision accommodates evidence when the policy alone cannot determine the decision. We evaluate a 0.6B implementation against 14 baselines on three datasets covering authentication, instruction hierarchy, and executable authorization rules. TrustJev achieves the highest mean Balance, the harmonic mean of accuracy on non-attack and attack inputs, exceeding the strongest baseline on each by 0.57-1.05 percentage points. In the authorization benchmark, it correctly answers all 32 attacks per source in a fixed pool for 95.77% of sources, compared with 80.98% for the strongest comparator. TrustJev maintains comparable inference memory and median latency, with additional overhead primarily during training.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Single-document corpus poisoning under black-box access poses a severe threat to Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). However, existing attacks predominantly rely on explicit factual refutations or imperative instruction overrides. Modern aligned LLMs often arbitrate such conflicts conservatively, rejecting overt contradictions in favor of parametric memory or clean retrieved context while triggering input guardrails. We propose MONOLOGUE, a single-document poisoning framework based on Logic Hijacking without explicit refutation. Rather than disputing the ground truth, our method concedes it as an authoritative historical baseline before introducing the update, weaving a grounded record-correction narrative (e.g., dated institutional reattributions) that presents the counterfactual target as the most recent update. To prevent suspicious live writes, payloads are refined entirely offline via local multi-dimensional gated optimization, coupling automated text quality audits with surrogate behavioral verification. Evaluations across three benchmarks, three retrieval backends, and four open-weight generator regimes show that our method achieves the strongest overall attack efficacy among the evaluated single-document poisoning attacks.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems can exhibit emergent behaviors through interaction, yet whether a similar phenomenon exists for reasoning alignment remains largely unexplored. We study whether agent interaction produces additional reference-grounded reasoning alignment beyond matched individual self-refinement, and introduce REMAP (Reference-Entailment-Matrix for Agentic Process), a framework that quantifies this effect through Semantic Coverage and interaction-specific alignment gains. Across diverse model families and two multi-hop reasoning benchmarks, we observe broadly positive interaction gains, particularly on reasoning content that depends on integrating evidence distributed across agents. These gains are consistently associated with independently measured peer-information recovery, while further analysis reveals composition-dependent heterogeneity effects and asymmetric information transfer across agent capability levels. Motivated by these findings, we further introduce a fact-level multi-agent communication framework that replaces full reasoning-trace exchange with compact, gap-aware, and selectively routed information. Our experiments show that compact fact exchange improves reasoning alignment and task performance while reducing communication overhead, whereas more selective communication exposes a trade-off between acquiring distributed information, retaining local content, and communication efficiency. Overall, our results show that collective emergent alignment can be quantified, linked to information-transfer patterns, and used to guide more effective multi-agent communication.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Visual counting benchmarks report whether vision-language models produce the correct number, but not whether errors arise from perception or reasoning. We introduce DecoyCount, a benchmark built from 1,660 real-world images with designed decoys across seven mechanisms, along with paired prompts: a neutral question, a matched alignment cue, and a novel multi-hop, multi-image question that situates the same counting problem in a realistic scenario. Using this design, we find that counting failures are induced partly by visual misalignment: models can execute the intended counting process when cue-guided but fail to select it unaided, and we term this difference in accuracy the reasoning-alignment gap. Despite being highly capable on general reasoning, frontier models remain below 50\% accuracy on our benchmark, while alignment gaps range from 11.8-22.1 percentage points; strikingly, we observe that more capable models, greater thinking budgets, and agentic processes often induce larger gaps. Because humans may bring context that models lack, we test whether textual underspecification contributes to misalignment using a scenario-based multi-hop condition without explicit hinting, but this added context does not improve aggregate performance. Our analysis concludes that stronger general reasoning does not by itself generalize to aligned visual reasoning, leaving misalignment with human visual expectations as a substantial component of the remaining deficit.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities across tasks, but they still struggle with complex reasoning that requires integrating scattered knowledge. Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) addresses this challenge by organizing fragment knowledge as graphs, while recent Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) further improve GraphRAG with graph-native retrieval and reasoning capabilities. However, existing GraphRAG methods usually adopt decoupled retrieve-then-generate pipelines, which create objective misalignment between retrieval and reasoning, and training GFM retrievers typically depends on expensive human annotations. To address these limitations, we propose \ourmethod, a unified end-to-end GraphRAG framework that tightly couples a GFM retriever with an LLM generator. Given a query, the GFM retriever first reasons over the graph to score and retrieve relevant document nodes. These node embeddings are then passed through a fully differentiable graph bridge, which projects them into soft-weighted graph prefixes according to retrieval scores and combines them with raw document texts to form graph-augmented prompts. In this way, structural information is injected directly into LLM generation, while the final answer loss can be back-propagated through the bridge to jointly optimize retrieval and reasoning. To stabilize training and reduce reliance on costly annotations, we further introduce a semantic-guided distillation module that provides auxiliary supervision using pseudo-labels derived from query-document semantic similarities. Extensive experiments on three multi-hop QA datasets and three out-of-domain GraphRAG benchmarks show that \ourmethod consistently outperforms strong baseline methods and generalizes well, which demonstrates the effectiveness of end-to-end GraphRAG with tightly integrated GFM retrieval and LLM reasoning.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Learned policies for classical planning act without search, but each is tied to the domain it was trained on: network modules per action schema, parameters per predicate, or vocabulary-sized encodings mean a trained policy cannot even be executed on a new domain's predicates and action schemas. We present GADAR (Generalizing Across Domains via Action Ranking), a policy that takes the domain description as input, π(s, G, D), and constructs grounded actions in domains never seen in training, without search. Symbols are described by the roles they play in the domain rather than by their names, in three layers that each extend the previous one: (i) a lifted domain layer embeds D in every state graph through structural signatures; (ii) a binding layer ties each ground action's applicability to the lifted preconditions it instantiates; and (iii) a chain layer states which schema produces what another needs and how far each schema stands from the goal. The same network therefore runs on every domain, seen or unseen. We evaluate zero-shot generalization leave-one-domain-out over eight benchmark domains. A single GADAR policy trained on seven domains solves 58.3% of their test instances, one point below the 59.3% of dedicated single-domain GADAR models, so one policy serves many domains at no cost in coverage; a multi-task control that keeps symbol identity in its weights, while strong on some of the domains it trains on, transfers to none of the held-out ones. Zero-shot, GADAR executes on every held-out domain and exceeds random choice under the same executor on the large test instances of all eight: it solves 22.6% of them on average, against 3.3% for random choice and 0.6% for the same representation without the chain layer, and reaches 68% on held-out Visitall. Zero-shot coverage nonetheless remains well below in-domain coverage on most domains, and we analyze where it is weakest.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Objective: The rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs) in healthcare has opened new possibilities for AI-assisted clinical decision-making; however, safe and effective deployment remains challenging because of hallucination and domain misalignment. This study introduces DocTalk, a doctor-assistive question-answering (QA) system designed to generate concise, chart-grounded responses from de-identified discharge summaries. Methods: We developed a high-fidelity data generation pipeline that produces 500 clinically relevant QA pairs through sentence-level decomposition, chain-of-thought-guided question formulation, and expert review. Six open-source LLMs, including three general-purpose and three biomedical models, were evaluated before and after parameter-efficient instruction tuning using Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). The framework integrates prompt engineering, in-context learning, and medically aligned evaluation to support factual and interpretable responses. Results: On DocTalk-QA, instruction tuning produced consistent improvements across lexical, semantic, and clinical fidelity metrics. The best-performing tuned model, Llama-3-Med42-8B, achieved ROUGE-L = 0.40, BERTScore-F1 = 0.76, and UMLS-F1 = 0.49. To examine external generalization beyond the DocTalk-QA dataset, the two best-performing models, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Llama-3-Med42-8B, were evaluated on PubMedQA before and after instruction tuning. Accuracy improved from 0.931 to 0.977 for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and from 0.935 to 0.943 for Llama-3-Med42-8B. Conclusion: DocTalk was deployed as a secure web-based platform for interactive EHR querying. These findings suggest that clinically grounded instruction tuning can improve medical QA performance and support practical clinical decision support.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Disease diagnosis is a key application of large language model (LLM) agents in healthcare. However, existing medical agents are primarily trained and evaluated with static question-answering data, providing limited opportunities to learn active information acquisition, examination decision-making, and dynamic clinical interaction. To address this limitation, we present ClinVerse, an interactive, real-data-grounded multi-agent environment designed for training and evaluating medical diagnostic agents. Built on ClinPatient, a scalable dataset comprising 150k diagnostic cases derived from real-world electronic health records, ClinVerse supports coordinated interactions among six specialized clinical roles. Specifically, a doctor agent starts from an initial patient complaint, progressively gathers clinical evidence through patient interviews and examination requests, and ultimately produces a diagnosis. For each interaction trajectory, a rule-based scoring framework quantifies information acquisition, examination ordering, and diagnostic accuracy. These trajectory-level scores serve as reward signals for agent training while also enabling scalable evaluation. In experiments, we evaluate various general-purpose and medical-domain models and show that nine self-evolution methods across three categories consistently improve agents' diagnostic capabilities within ClinVerse. We further analyze the interplay among model capability, environment feedback, and self-evolution methods.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reasoning language models generate thousands of tokens per answer, and generation speed is limited by memory bandwidth, so storing weights in two bits instead of bfloat16 can potentially make inference 8$\times$ cheaper. Two-bit methods are usually judged by perplexity and multiple-choice question-answering accuracy, both of which say little about whether a model can still reason through more difficult problems. We present ThinkBit, a strictly scalar two-bit framework built to preserve reasoning, in which every weight is an integer codeword times a bfloat16 scale shared by a group of weights. We first use a sensitivity metric to assign each layer a ternary, 2-bit, or 3-bit tier to allocate bitwidths optimally among layers. This is followed by a layerwise Gumbel-softmax-based scalar quantization algorithm that searches for optimal codewords block by block. Since a layerwise scheme does not capture how quantized layers interact with each other, we recalibrate scales, with the codewords frozen, using a knowledge-distillation-based loss against the teacher model. We then perform a short quantization-aware training (QAT) stage that lets the model mimic the base model's distribution by reassigning codewords. Finally, a paired reasoning test on training problems selects the final checkpoint between the recalibration stage and the QAT stage. On Qwen3-1.7B at matched size, our recalibrated checkpoint scores 46.7 on MATH-500 against 30.0 for EfficientQAT trained on the same data, and 73.0 after QAT on reasoning traces; at 0.52 GB it is smaller than every published 2-bit GGUF of the model yet more accurate on MATH-500. We show that this framework scales and up to 14B, a 3.74 GB model scores 94.8 on MATH-500, against 97.5 for the 29.5 GB bfloat16 model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Physical reasoning underpins how embodied systems understand and act in the physical world, yet vision-language models (VLMs) remain unreliable on questions about dynamics and physical causality. Progress is critically constrained by the scarcity of grounded counterfactual training data, as observational videos capture only the factual trajectory that occurred. The self-evolving paradigm offers a route beyond fixed datasets by enabling models to construct their own training problems. Extending this paradigm to physics, however, requires an external source of truth that can both validate model-proposed environments and verify alternative physical outcomes under intervention. We introduce \physevolve{}, a verifier-grounded self-evolving framework that trains VLMs for causal physical reasoning from model-proposed executable worlds and simulation-authored tasks. At each round, a shared VLM proposes executable world programs and evidence schemas; a deterministic physics engine freezes each valid world, simulates matched baseline and body-elided counterfactual branches, and synthesizes discrete, answer-bound visual tasks. Exact verifier feedback from task solving updates both the shared policy via GRPO and an adaptive curriculum over physical intents. Starting from Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, \physevolve{} achieves targeted gains across PhysBench, CausalPhys, and ContPhy without human intervention data, improving PhysBench Throwing by $8.8$ points and ContPhy Counterfactual Removal and Goal-Pit by $7.4$ and $24.4$ points. These gains concentrate on interaction-sensitive and altered-world questions, demonstrating zero-shot transfer of causal principles to unseen complex dynamics (cloth, fluid, soft bodies) and real-world video.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Fact-verification benchmarks score the final label, while an interactive verifier must first decide whether the available evidence is sufficient. We focus on that intermediate Stop-or-Clarify Decision. Uncertainty alone is insufficient, because the benefit of another evidence-acquisition step is unknown before the step is taken. Sequential Experience-Adaptive Clarification (SEAC) wraps a fixed verifier with a lightweight evidence-acquisition policy. For each claim, SEAC retrieves evidence, runs the verifier, forms a label-free decision state, and combines stop risk with an observational clarification-utility estimate from earlier executed trajectories. Clarification is requested only when this score exceeds the action cost and decision threshold. On SciFact, LIAR, and FEVER, SEAC improves mean accuracy and macro-F1 over the compared fact-verification and active-retrieval baselines. Equal-budget and formula-level diagnostics support selective clarification as a contributor to the gain, while the small SciFact split leaves its paired improvement inconclusive.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Decision needs vary with each application, language, and deployment environment. The Decision Model Factory turns those needs into specialized, sovereign models on demand. A brief recipe guides the training, calibration, verification, and evaluation of a selected backbone, delivering a deployable decision model under the operator's control. The same workflow creates a new specialist whenever the requirements change. Across Japanese decision tasks, compact deployment, and long-context passage ranking, five factory-built models improve upon their backbones; three surpass the strongest results in a fixed public Japanese benchmark comparison. A dedicated runner computes only permitted-answer probabilities and reuses shared context, more than doubling serving speed on matched multi-question workloads with essentially unchanged decision scores. Together, the factory and runner make fast, task-specific decision models practical to build and deploy whenever needed.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Despite the remarkable capabilities of large language models (LLMs) across diverse applications, they remain vulnerable to generating content that violates safety regulations and policies. To mitigate these risks, LLMs undergo safety alignment; however, they can still be effectively jailbroken. Off-the-shelf guardrail models are commonly deployed to monitor generations, but these models primarily focus on detection rather than ensuring safe decoding of LLM outputs. Moreover, existing efforts lack rigorous safety guarantees, which are crucial for the universal deployment of LLMs and certifiable compliance with regulatory standards. In this paper, we propose a Claim-based Stream Decoding (CSD) algorithm coupled with a statistical risk guarantee framework using conformal analysis. Specifically, our CSD algorithm integrates a stream guardrail model to safeguard sequential claims generated by LLMs and incorporates a backtracking mechanism to revise claims flagged with high safety risks. We provide theoretical guarantees demonstrating that the CSD algorithm achieves the desired generation distribution subject to safety constraints. Furthermore, we introduce a generation risk certification framework and derive a high-probability upper bound on the safety risk of the proposed CSD algorithm. We prove that our method can asymptotically control safety risk to any desired level. Empirical evaluations demonstrate the effectiveness and efficiency of the CSD algorithm compared to state-of-the-art safety decoding approaches. Additionally, we validate the soundness and tightness of the derived safety risk upper bound using realistic data.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Modern large language models (LLMs) often exhibit overconfident errors, assigning high confidence to incorrect predictions and thereby undermining reliability in real-world use. Existing calibration methods typically rely on post-hoc adjustment or retraining, operating at the output level without addressing the underlying causes of miscalibration. We propose CoCHI, (Class-oriented Causal Head Intervention), a framework that improves calibration by identifying and intervening on internal components responsible for distinct confidence behaviours. By analysing predictions through correctness-confidence patterns, CoCHI enables targeted intervention on model internals, reducing overconfident errors while preserving correct predictions. Our approach provides a mechanistic perspective on calibration, linking reliability to identifiable structures within the model rather than treating it as an output-level issue. Experiments across multiple LLMs show consistent improvements in calibration metrics, including Expected Calibration Error, Negative Log-Likelihood, and Brier Score, while maintaining accuracy on five multiple-choice question answering benchmarks. These results suggest that effective calibration requires not only adjusting outputs, but also addressing the underlying causal mechanisms within the model.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Deep narrative understanding requires distinguishing what happens from how it is presented, moving beyond the chronological fabula to decode the sjuzet, the discursive organization through which themes are staged. We study this through the recurrence of literary motifs (blood in Macbeth, for instance), where symbolic significance emerges not from local cues, but through repetition-with-variation across the whole work. We operationalize this setting as Computational Motif Discovery, a transductive task that predicts missing line-motif links using the full narrative structure of a play. We propose Critic's Eye, which models the work as a heterogeneous Narrative Topology Graph and performs discriminative inference over global structural evidence. Critic's Eye achieves 84.8% Hit@5 on our benchmark, nearly quadrupling state-of-the-art proprietary foundation models (Gemini-3-pro-preview, ~22.6%). Our analysis reveals that despite massive scaling, sequence-based models hit a "semantic ceiling", struggling to resolve dispersed dependencies. These findings suggest that explicit structural priors are a far more effective inductive bias than parameter scaling for decoding the complex architecture of long-form literature.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Long-horizon LLM agents interact with their environments through complete assistant turns while generating each action token by token. We observe that critic values vary primarily across turns, motivating optimization anchored at turns with finer credit allocation among tokens. We propose Multiscale Policy Optimization (MPO), combining Multiscale Advantage Estimation (MAE) and turn-level importance sampling to align credit assignment and policy updates at the turn scale. Extensive experiments across search, tool-use, and automation tasks with 4B and 30B-A3B models demonstrate consistent gains over strong RL baselines.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Patent practice lies at the intersection of technical, legal, and linguistic work, making it challenging yet crucial to technological innovation. Recently, rapid advances in large language models (LLMs) and agentic systems have demonstrated the potential to complete realistic business workflows and even patent works. This raises a question: can current AI systems perform patent work reliably? Existing benchmarks cover a limited range of patent tasks and simplify evidence acquisition by providing reference materials or candidate documents. In this paper, we introduce PatentAgentBench, a benchmark comprising 510 instances across eight task suites: novelty search, freedom-to-operate (FTO) search, design FTO, invalidity search, claim charting, patent drafting, office-action response, and patent translation. Curated from real patent records and professional materials, the benchmark combines task-specific metrics with expert-reviewed annotations and rubrics. Retrieval tasks such novelty search, FTO, etc., require agents to search and compare patent collections without a supplied candidate pool. The drafting and OAR tasks assess the quality of agents' work given technical disclosure and office action notice. We evaluate seven LLMs across four agent harnesses on PatentAgentBench, finding that current systems still struggle to identify novelty, assess risk, and understand practitioner intent throughout patent workflows.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated for fairness, yet most existing benchmarks audit one sensitive attribute at a time. This single-attribute paradigm can miss intersectional harms, where multiple attributes interact non-additively and produce failures that are invisible under marginal evaluation. We introduce ComFair, a configurable benchmark that converts existing fairness data into 479,066 controlled Dual- and Multi-attribute instances and pairs every augmented instance with its task-equivalent source. Across Recognition, Selection, Continuation, and Conversation, this pairing supports a unified audit of performance or bias-score shifts, how often added attributes introduce new errors or mask existing ones, and model-ranking stability. Experiments on ten LLMs show task- and subgroup-dependent sensitivity: discriminative tasks expose both transition directions even when aggregate changes are modest, generative tasks exhibit larger and directionally asymmetric shifts, and rankings vary with attribute composition. ComFair therefore provides a scalable controlled sensitivity audit---rather than a model of the natural joint distribution of identities---for testing the robustness of fairness-benchmark conclusions beyond single-attribute settings.

</details>

Abstract (英文): A graph neural network (GNN) forecasting a collection of load series combines two mechanisms, sharing parameters across series and reading neighboring series, and a gain over a non-graph model may come from either; telling them apart decides whether a graph is worth maintaining. We propose a model diagnostic protocol that compares the GNN with three reference models: a per-node network, a network shared across nodes without neighbors, and the same GNN without edges. Degree-preserving rewiring tests whether neighbor identity matters. After a tuned benchmark on regional load in France and on low-voltage feeders in the UK, we apply the protocol to deliberately short node histories, as when non-stationary demand leaves only recent observations relevant: the feeders, within one week, and a public dataset of client consumption series. On the feeders, sharing parameters recovers most of the error gap between node-specific models and GNNs, about two thirds against a regularized one. Adding the edges to an otherwise identical backbone brings a smaller gain, half or more of which disappears under rewiring. On the client series, the neighbor mean degrades the per-node model on the shorter window and the edges' gain is not consistently distinguishable from zero; on the regions of France, where each node's own history is already informative, the ranking between per-node models and GNNs changes with tuning. A synthetic study points to noisy local measurements as one regime where neighbors help. In an exploratory online aggregation, graph and non-graph forecasters appear complementary, and the weight assigned to GNNs is not proportional to their contribution. In none of the three datasets do the edges account for most of a GNN's advantage over node-specific models, so a forecasting gain should be credited to the graph only after comparison with per-node and shared references.

</details>

Abstract (英文): We investigate whether limbic-like signatures of rapid, salience-driven behavior can be identified in high-resolution Counter-Strike 2 telemetry. This exploratory study introduces Ego-Knowledge, a leak-reduced reconstruction of each player's plausible perceptual state, supported by a millisecond-precision visibility engine and a reaction-centered extraction pipeline. Using a neuroscience-motivated 60--120 ms window, we derive coarse fight-or-flight labels from visibility- and damage-triggered events, with back-damage cases enabling constrained reaction-time analysis and manual label checks. We train two telemetry-native models using only information available by the trigger: EMBER (gradient boosting) and ORBIT (a custom transformer). On the full validation set, EMBER achieves 73.5% accuracy and ORBIT 71.2%, versus a 51.5% majority-class baseline, and both generalize to held-out players. The strongest LLM baseline achieves 62.7% on 1,000 validation cases; on these same cases, EMBER (73.6%) and ORBIT (70.9%) perform better (McNemar p=1.3e-9 and 1.5e-6, respectively), with orders-of-magnitude lower inference time and cost, though the comparison is not compute-matched. Including a short post-trigger window changes forecasting into early response recognition, raising accuracy monotonically to 85.15%. We propose this curve as a candidate probe of the transition from rapid to deliberative control, but roughly uniform per-tick gains do not establish that interpretation. The neuroscience framing is hypothesis-generating, not mechanistic: physiological ground truth, suitable control distributions, and reverse-inference limitations remain open. We outline extensions to auditory reconstruction, larger datasets, and telemetry-native foundation models.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The recent incident in which OpenAI agents autonomously compromised Hugging Face's infrastructure illustrates the importance of instruction hierarchy (IH): an LLM should follow lower-priority instructions, such as user requests, only when they do not conflict with higher-priority instructions, such as system policies. IH can be effectively improved by fine-tuning LLMs on simulated examples of instruction conflicts, whose diversity is key to teaching LLMs to properly handle conflicts at test time. However, our decomposition of a task into goals and constraints reveals a conflict type -- goal-goal conflicts -- that is absent from existing IH training sets from OpenAI and NVIDIA. We show the importance of this conflict type: fine-tuning Qwen3.5-9B and Gemma4-12B-it on examples of this conflict type significantly improves IH on a wide range of real-world tasks unseen in training; adding these examples to existing IH training sets for fine-tuning also gives better IH adherence. The IH gains come with trivial mean utility changes on no-conflict tasks across eight benchmarks. Our work advocates including this new conflict type in IH training, even if these examples do not reflect typical LLM use.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Multimodal video retrieval combines visual, acoustic, and speech information, yet how their fusion order should depend on the data and the retrieval query remains unclear. We introduce Unified Progressive Representation Learning for Order Analysis, or UniPRO, to investigate this question through configurable fusion schedules with matched model components and training settings. Starting from text-to-video retrieval, we find that the tested one-by-one schedules outperform stacked fusion, while changing the order within one-by-one fusion also changes performance. To guide order selection, we examine two complementary considerations: learning difficulty under unequal modality availability and preservation of information relevant to the query. Complete-modality controls show that missing inputs alone cannot explain order sensitivity. At R@1, text and audio retrieval favor schedules that introduce the modality matching the query last. Controlled speech missingness during training and testing reverses the preferred order in both R@1 and SumR, with different crossover points. These findings motivate balancing the two considerations when selecting a fusion path. We then use them to predict a visual-last schedule for image queries, which achieves 97.9 R@1, the highest among the evaluated orders on MSR-VTT-9k. Main experiments on four text-to-video benchmarks establish the effectiveness of UniPRO, with component ablations assessing its supporting design. Together, the results identify fusion order as a task-dependent choice that should account jointly for modality availability and the retrieval query.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Evaluating human-AI co-design requires testing whether coding agents can translate real users' multimodal requests into working application revisions. We introduce Co-Design Bench, a reference-free benchmark of 273 tasks curated from 37,463 deduplicated queries across 26,314 real user coding sessions. Its two tracks, Multimodal Editing (ME) and Repository Editing (RE), cover localized edits with optional image, audio, video, or document inputs and coordinated repository revisions. Tasks span three capability dimensions: Grounded Understanding, UI Transformation, and Functional Implementation. The tasks pair normalized requests and runnable pre-edit applications with 2,331 hidden expert-authored acceptance criteria. Our judge-then-refute protocol grades criteria using source and browser evidence, challenges positive verdicts with a separate LLM verifier, and audits visual and interaction claims without gold code or target screenshots. Under a shared agentic harness, the strongest of ten model endpoints passes an average of 69.9% of criteria per task but fully satisfies only 26.4% of tasks. All ten endpoints achieve lower criterion completion on repository edits spanning three or four change types than on those spanning one or two. Co-Design Bench provides a testbed for studying the gap between partial requirement satisfaction and complete task success under agentic execution for real-user application revisions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Long-horizon web search requires LLM-based agents to integrate information across web pages through extended interactions under task-specific constraints. Evaluating this capability is important not only for measuring final answer quality, but also for assessing whether an agent acquires, verifies, and uses evidence reliably over extended interactions. Existing evaluations, however, mainly rely on simulated trajectories or on post-hoc trajectories reconstructed from final answers, rather than on retained real interaction traces, which limits the fidelity of behavioral assessment and weakens support for fine-grained error localization. To address this gap, we introduce LONGSEARCHEVAL, a trajectory-aware agent evaluation framework that combines a unified agent execution protocol with source constrained outcome verification and retrospective failure-node localization. Stage 1 verifies answer groups against agent-cited webpages without human-authored gold answers; Stage 2 audits normalized rounds and links flagged failure nodes to incorrect answer groups. We evaluate 16 models from eight families on 100 tasks, yielding 1,600 retained runs and 6,545 automatically flagged failure nodes. Trajectory collection and evaluation required 1,037 and 318 hours of cumulative runtime, respectively. Human validation yields 94.50% agreement on supplied answer-group labels and 98.11% feature-correspondence precision for audited round-group links. Together, outcome scores and linked trajectory evidence support controlled model comparison and retrospective inspection of answer-related content.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Designing building energy systems is a high-dimensional, mixed-integer search problem: when each design is evaluated by a year-long simulation, black-box optimizers typically need hundreds of evaluations. This paper presents AutoEnergyDesign, a framework in which a Large Language Model (LLM) agent reasons over previous simulation results, proposes configuration changes and learns in context from each outcome, while a git-based mechanism automatically reverts unsuccessful changes. The agent couples a reasoning model with an explicit memory organisation (procedural, semantic, episodic and reflective), a constrained action interface and an environment-grounded keep/revert signal. The simulator covers eight technologies (photovoltaics, micro-wind, combined heat and power, solar thermal, gas boilers, batteries, heat pumps and inverters) and computes the 25-year Net Present Cost (NPC), including capital and operating costs and salvage value. On a case study with measured building load profiles, the agent, run with Claude Haiku 5.5, Sonnet 5.5 and Opus 5.5 (ten runs of 50 simulations each), is compared with random search, Tree-structured Parzen Estimator (TPE) Bayesian optimization and a $(1+1)$ evolution strategy sharing the agent's acceptance rule (300 evaluations, ten seeds). With all three models the agent reaches an NPC of about 3.53 MEUR, 1.20% below the best TPE result and 0.23% below the best evolution-strategy result. It comes within 1% of this design after a median of 18 (Opus) to 27.5 (Haiku) simulations, whereas among the baselines only the evolution strategy does so, in half of its seeds and after a median of 192 evaluations. Opus reaches the best design in all ten runs, Sonnet in six (two runs never come within 1%), and Haiku comes within 1% in every run but reaches it exactly only once. Every decision is recorded in an auditable trace.

</details>

Abstract (英文): AI systems built around large models increasingly use experience and feedback to make persistent changes to models, prompts, memories, tools, and workflows. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) extends this process to inductive biases and to the mechanisms that govern future updates, which can themselves be revised and reused across improvement cycles. Yet self-modification alone does not guarantee trustworthy improvement. Reported gains may reflect flawed evaluations, previously acquired capabilities may deteriorate, and compliance with behavioral constraints may weaken. Compromised artifacts or sensitive information may also persist in successor systems. Changes to improvement mechanisms may invalidate earlier verification results and make human oversight less effective. This survey presents an operational definition and process framework for RSI and organizes the requirements for trustworthy RSI into four interconnected themes: genuine and sustained improvement; behavioral constraints, alignment, and fairness; system security and privacy; and transparency and controllability. We examine these themes from three perspectives: Improvement Validity, Constraint Preservation, and Effective Oversight. This analysis covers improvement diagnosis, proposal generation, proposal evaluation, and update implementation (S1--S4), including the recursive application of these stages to improvement mechanisms (RI). We review risks, safeguards, and evaluation evidence for individual updates, effects inherited by successor systems, and revisions to improvement mechanisms, while distinguishing direct RSI findings from evidence in related systems. Finally, we examine evaluation settings, identify requirements for independent and longitudinal assessment, and discuss applications and open research problems.

</details>

Abstract (英文): News outlets covering the same event differ in what they emphasize and omit. \textit{Same Story, Different Lens} is an experimental research prototype for comparing these choices. It collects world and politics coverage from 17 outlets, groups reports about the same event, and produces structured analyses of sentiment, political lean, framing, loaded language, possible omissions, and model-estimated proportions of factual claims and opinion. Loaded-language entries include quotations and neutral rewordings. Three model roles (agents) handle article analysis, assessment of source support, and cross-outlet briefing generation. Analyses are stored in a JSONL archive. The evaluation compares outlet-level lean estimates with approximate, predefined reference ratings and summarizes the Critic's model-assigned grounding scores. These measures provide preliminary diagnostics. A grounding score reflects the model's assessment of support in the supplied text and does not establish factual accuracy or constitute independent verification.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Generative recommendation with semantic IDs (SIDs) represents each item as a sequence of discrete tokens and autoregressively predicts the next item's SID. Despite strong recommendation performance, repeated decoding over multiple SID positions incurs substantial inference cost. Knowledge distillation transfers knowledge from a powerful generative teacher to a lightweight student, yet conventional objectives do not fully account for multi-token SID generation. We identify two limitations: item-level distribution alignment may not adequately preserve the teacher's ranking of candidate groups sharing SID prefixes, while uniform alignment across SID positions may affect final item ranking differently. We propose MuSID-KD, which combines Hierarchical Candidate Distribution Alignment (HCDA) and Ranking Impact Guided Levelwise Distillation (RIGD). HCDA aggregates item probabilities according to shared SID prefixes and aligns candidate distributions at successive levels, from candidate groups to individual items. RIGD evaluates the reduction in pairwise ranking surrogate loss when one student score contribution is replaced with the teacher's contribution, and allocates supervision accordingly. Experiments on real-world recommendation datasets using three lightweight student architectures demonstrate improved recommendation accuracy and better alignment of prefix marginal distributions and pairwise item rankings, without modifying the student's online inference architecture. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/m7k2-review-83f9/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Reasoning-tuned and reward-trained models are often assumed to verify more reliably because they reason longer or receive correctness signals. We test this in evidence-sufficiency judgment: deciding whether available information actually supports a causal claim, a domain with no decidable correctness reward. Using a pre-registered, same-base, controlled knock-in design, we compare a boundary-keeping recipe (SFT then GRPO) with its own baseline, a scrambled-pairing placebo, and a general-purpose reasoning model under an action-pressure protocol with no evaluation invitation. All load-bearing outcomes are rated by two separate model-rating sessions masked to arm labels; automatic judges only trigger. The trained arm raises spontaneous correct refusal from 25% to 100% (40 per arm; Wilson 95% CI lower bound 91.2%, non-overlapping with the baseline interval [14.2%, 40.2%]), while the scrambled placebo collapses (5% spontaneous / 7.5% invited correct; 91.1% false refusal). The effect is boundary-specific rather than blanket refusal: automatic false refusal on 281 well-formed claims is 1.1% (Wilson upper bound 3.09%), and a targeted 18-item arm-(c) recheck (3 flagged plus 15 accepted) yields 0/18 and 1/18 false refusals in two sessions. The dissociation is the main result: final boundary-keeping is trainable without an inference-time verifier, while observed active numeric/calculation checking in the H3 assay remains near zero; SFT saturation makes the GRPO increment not separately identifiable. A pre-registered mechanism adjudication favors reward internalization of final-action status (H2, best-supported, not proven). The scope is a controlled 14B same-family study: we do not claim proof, cross-family generality, or independent human-expert validation; the rating sessions are model sessions, not human reviewers.

</details>
